Holonomic Bessel modules and generating functions
[

Abstract
. We have solved a number of holonomic PDEs derived from the Bessel modules which are related to the generating functions of classical Bessel functions and the difference Bessel functions recently discovered by Bohner and Cuchta. This D D -module approach both unifies and extends generating functions of the classical and the difference Bessel functions. It shows that the algebraic structures of the Bessel modules and related modules determine the possible formats of Bessel’s generating functions studied in this article. As a consequence of these D D -modules structures, a number of new recursion formulae, integral representations and new difference Bessel polynomials have been discovered. The key ingredients of our argument involve new transmutation formulae related to the Bessel modules and the construction of D D -linear maps between different appropriately constructed submodules. This work can be viewed as D D -module approach to Truesdell’s F F -equation theory specialised to Bessel functions. The framework presented in this article can be applied to other special functions.



Y. M. Chiang]Yik Man Chiang

A. Ching]Avery Ching

X. Lin]Xiaoli Lin

\dedication

keywords
D
-modules, Bessel modules, Generating functions, Difference Bessel polynomials, Glaisher’s modules, difference Bessel polynomials.

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 32S40, 33E99, 30D10; Secondary 47B37 , 47B47, 12H05, 12H10, 13N10.

1.
Introduction

Generating functions play important roles in both pure and applied mathematics
[
27
,
67
]
. In particular, generating functions of Bessel functions play pivotal roles in the mathematical formulation of electromagnetic wave and acoustic scattering problems, see for examples,
[
7
,
23
]
. Other application includes the study of Rogers-Ramanujan identities
[
24
,
25
]
.
In this paper, we present a systematic study of generating functions of Bessel functions based on the Bessel module (
1.2
) defined below. This is the first of a series of papers originates from our study of generating functions of classical special functions using
D
-module methodology. It is known that
D
-modules is an efficient language in exhibiting algebraic structures and in carrying out explicit computation of identities of special functions in general, see for examples
[
14
,
15
,
24
,
25
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
46
,
54
,
68
,
70
]
. We demonstrate that this is indeed the case that the
D
-modules approach not only allows us to better understand some of the classical formulae about special functions but also to derive their new difference analogues. This work can be considered as a continuation of the previous works of
[
43
,
44
,
51
,
60
,
58
,
61
,
62
]
and to a less extent of
[
8
,
24
,
25
]
by algebraic methods.
A noteworthy feature of this study is that the main results are first obtained in the
D
-modules framework before their
D
-modules structures are being manifested in complex domains differently. Thus the study unifies the classical Bessel functions and the recently discovered difference Bessel functions amongst other possible manifestations. The fact that the main results have been derived entirely within the appropriate
D
-modules that is subject to different manifestations in the complex plane is a vindication of the viewpoint from umbral calculus discovered centuries ago.

1.1.
Bessel’s modules

We extend Bessel’s classical generating function
1

1

It was known to Hansen in 1845 and earlier to Jacobi in the 1836 in a modified form
[
64

.

e^{\frac{x}{2}\big(t-\frac{1}{t}\big)}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{n}(x)\,t^{n}

(1.1)

for the Bessel coefficients
(J_{n}(x))
which denotes the bilateral sequence of
Bessel functions of the first kind
of integer orders, to the case of non-integer orders
(J_{\nu+n}(x))
where
\nu\not=0

the quotient module

\mathcal{B}_{\nu}:=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}\partial_{1}+(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})-X_{1}X_{2})+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}\partial_{1}-(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})+{X_{1}}/{X_{2}})},

\nu\in\mathbb{C}
in this article, where the

\partial_{i},\,X_{i}\ (j=1,\,2)

[\partial_{j},X_{k}]=\delta_{j,k},\hskip 20.00003pt[\partial_{j},\partial_{k}]=0,\hskip 20.00003pt[X_{j},X_{k}]=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{for }j,\,k=1,\,2.

(1.3)

Here the two relations

X_{1}\partial_{1}+\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2}-X_{1}X_{2},\hskip 20.00003ptX_{1}\partial_{1}-(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})+{X_{1}}/{X_{2}},

(1.4)

about Bessel functions, known as Gauss’ contiguous relations

The method of derivation of the above results and other results in this paper is based on construction of
D
-linear maps from the Bessel modules
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
(and other
D
-modules) to appropriately
manifested

D
-module analytic function spaces of two variables (e.g.,
\mathcal{O}_{dd},\ \mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
). Other quotient
D
-modules related to the half-Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
studied in this paper includes
Bessel polynomial modules

\Theta,\mathcal{Y}
and
Glaisher modules

\mathcal{G}_{\pm}
which also are listed in Appendix §
C.3
. This paper can be considered as a
D
-module study of Truesdell’s
F
-equation theory
[
62
]
, which summarised and extended research works about generating functions of predecessors from the nineteenth century, when applied to Bessel’s generating functions.
9
9

9

The authors only got to know Truesdell’s work after obtaining the main results of this paper.
However, unlike the analytic approach used in
[
62
]
, the existence and uniqueness of the
F
-equation theory are replaced by the holonomicity of the
D
-modules concerned.
The paper aims to show that
D
-modules is not only a natural language but also an efficient computational tool to study special functions. We have built up our applications of
D
-modules that represent some of the simplest holonomic equations which give raise to the exponential, trigonometric functions, etc from scratch in order to deal with the quotient modules studied in this paper. This is because we could reach our main results efficiently with our self-contained approach instead of utilising the full force of
D
-modules theory from, for examples,
[
38
,
56
]
. This is especially the case when we derive difference analogues of the classical results. Closer to our approach but differ substantially in details were the pioneering works of Wilf and Zeilberger
[
70
,
68
]
in which the authors used holonomic
D
-modules methodology to derive hypergeometric type identities. Our approach is also different from the Lie algebraic approach of generating functions used by Weisner
[
65
]
, see also
[
47
]
.
The choice to start with Bessel functions is a natural one in regard to them being ubiquity in many research areas and wide range of applications, see for examples
[
2
,
26
,
43
,
63
,
66
,
64
]
.

Before we discuss the system (
1.9
), let us introduce the
difference Bessel function
of order
\nu
recently discovered by Bohner and Cuthta
[
4
]

J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)}(x)_{\nu+2k},

(1.17)

where
(x)_{\nu}=\Gamma(x+1)/\Gamma(x+1-\nu)
for an arbitrary
\nu
from our
D
-module perspective. Bohner and Cuthta
[
4
]
shows that the Newton series solves the
difference Bessel equation

x(x-1)\triangle^{2}y(x-2)+x\triangle y(x-1)+x(x-1)y(x-2)-\nu^{2}y(x)=0.

(1.18)

The first two authors and Tsang have shown in
[
13
]
that the function (
1.17
) and the equation (
1.18
) are manifestations of the Weyl-algebraic Bessel (
3.22
) and the Weyl-Bessel operator

(X\partial)^{2}+(X^{2}-\nu^{2})

in the analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, where the subscript
\Delta
indicates that the
\partial
in
\mathbb{C}\langle\partial,X\rangle
subjects to
[\partial,X]=\partial X-X\partial=1
, that is in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, is interpreted as a forward difference operator instead, that is,
\partial f(x)=\Delta f(x)=f(x+1)-f(x)
and
Xf(x)=xf(x-1)
(see Example
3.32
.). Indeed, one recovers the classical Bessel equation

x^{2}y^{\prime\prime}(x)+xy^{\prime}(x)+(x^{2}-\nu^{2})y(x)=0.

(1.19)

and classical Bessel function with the well-known manifestation
\partial f(x)=f^{\prime}(x),\ Xf(x)=xf(x)
.

The difference Bessel functions are not discussed in Nikifornov
et al

Before we discuss similar formulae that we have obtained for the new difference analogues of Bessel polynomials and Glaisher’s trigonometric type generating functions below, let us first return to the discussion mentioned in the first paragraph about the aspect of our Weyl-algebraic methodology in
solving
12
12

12

See Definition
3.1
.
the two “PDEs” (
1.4
) in the context of the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
. In fact, the process of a priori computation for the “generating functions” greatly simplifies the entire process of
finding the generating function that are classically written within suitably defined analytic function spaces. This a priori computation is therefore
no less significant
than the conventional approach done in a complex domain.

As a by-product of (
1.7
) of “extracting the residue” from the formula (
1.7
), we obtain the integral representation

J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)=e^{i\pi\nu}\dfrac{\sin(x-\nu)\pi}{\sin(\pi x)}\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}dt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\rm Re}(x)<\mathop{\rm Re}(\nu)

where the integration path is a Hankel-type contour (Theorem
3.45
). This integral representation is the difference analogue of the classical Schläfli-Sonine integral of Corollary
3.29

[
64
, p. 176]
. Thus, the classical Schläfli-Sonine integral can be considered as “residue extraction” from the formula (
1.6
).

The rest of this paper will focus on two main variants of the Bessel module of order half
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
. They are related to classical Glaisher’s trigonometric type generating functions and the classical Bessel polynomials. As a result we have also obtained their difference analogues. The solving of these variants of
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
(Theorem
4.22
, Theorem
6.8
, Theorem
7.4
, and Theorem
7.7
)
are more complicated in the sense that certain
quadratic extensions
on the base Weyl-algebra are needed.

1.2.
Glaisher’s modules

The treatment of Glaisher’s Poisson type generating functions
[
64
, p. 140]

\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi x}}\cos\sqrt{x^{2}-2xt}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}J_{n-\frac{1}{2}}(x)\,\frac{t^{n}}{n!},

which is valid for
2|t|<|x|
, requires a change of characteristics variables
to (
1.4
) with
\nu=-\frac{1}{2}
different from that for obtaining the (
1.11
), so that the alternative
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module, called the negative Glaisher’s module is given by

\mathcal{G}_{-}=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}\big(W_{1}\Xi_{1}+(W_{2}\Xi_{2}-\frac{1}{2})-W_{1}\Xi_{2}^{-1}\big)+\mathcal{A}_{2}\big(W_{1}\Xi_{1}-(W_{2}\Xi_{2}-\frac{1}{2})+W_{1}\Xi_{2}\big)},

in which the
\Xi_{1},W_{1}
and
\Xi_{2},\,W_{2}
play the roles of
\partial_{1},\,X_{1}
and
\partial_{2},\,X_{2}
respectively, as defined in (
1.3
).

As an example, after a “further change of variables” and solving the resulting PDEs finally results in the following difference analogue of Glaisher’s generating functions for difference Bessel functions with half-integer orders (Theorem
7.12
):

\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\dfrac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin(\pi x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}\frac{e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}\cos\sqrt{\lambda^{2}-2\lambda t}}{\Gamma(-x)}\,d\lambda=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}J^{\Delta}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}(x)\frac{t^{n}}{n!},

valid on the whole
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. In particular, the generating function is a solution to the system of delay-differential equations
13
13

13

These equations are generally called PDEs in a generalised sense in this paper.
(Theorem
7.10
)

\begin{split}&tf_{tt}(x,\,t)+\big(x+1/2\big)f_{t}(x,\,t)-xf_{t}(x-1,\,t)-xf(x-1,\,t)=0,\\
&tf_{t}(x,\,t)-xf_{t}(x-1,\,t)+xf(x-1,\,t)-\big(x+1/2\big)f(x,\,t)=0.\end{split}

(1.25)

1.3.
Bessel polynomial modules

Next we consider half Bessel-module
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
which is more natural to treat the classical reverse polynomials
\theta_{n}(x)
instead of the
y_{n}(x)=x^{n}\theta_{n}(1/x)
. If
X\partial
is replaced by
X\partial-X-1/2
in (
1.4
), then the
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
is replaced by the
reverse Bessel polynomial module

\Theta=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[\partial_{1}-1+X_{1}/\partial_{2}]+\mathcal{A}_{2}[X_{1}\partial_{1}-2X_{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}+\partial_{2}]}.

such that the two generators
\partial_{1}-1+X_{1}/\partial_{2}
and
X_{1}\partial_{1}-2X_{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}+\partial_{2}
would generate a third element

X_{1}\partial_{1}^{2}-2(X_{1}+X_{2}\partial_{2})\partial_{1}+2X_{2}\partial_{2}
which is a Weyl-algebraic analogue of the classical Bessel polynomial equation. In particular, we have derived the difference equation (Theorem
4.17
)

(x-2n)\,\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x+1)-4(x-n)\,\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x)+3x\,\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x-1)=0,

(1.26)

which is satisfied by
difference reverse Bessel (Newton) polynomial of degree

n

\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{2^{k}(n-k)!\,k!}\,(x)_{n-k}.

In a similar fashion, the two generators
\partial_{1}-1+X_{1}/\partial_{2}
and
X_{1}\partial_{1}-2X_{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}+\partial_{2}
implies two delay-difference equations for difference reversed Bessel polynomials for each
n
(Theorem
4.18
):

\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x+1)-2\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)+x\theta^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-1)=0,

and

\theta^{\Delta}_{n+1}(x)+(x-2n-1)\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)-2x\,\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x-1)=0.

A “characteristic change of variables” to the module
\Theta
happens to be
\rho^{2}=1-2X_{2}
and we formulate a “quadratic extension of
\Theta
” in Theorem
6.6

\Theta(\rho):=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)[-\rho^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}]+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)[X_{1}\partial_{1}+\rho^{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}]}.

so that the commutative diagram of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear maps enable us to derive a generating function for the difference reverse Bessel polynomials

\frac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2\pi i\sin\pi x\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x)\,\frac{t^{n}}{n!}

that is valid in
\mathbb{C}\times B(0,\,\frac{1}{2})
in Theorem
6.8
. The generating function for the difference reverse Bessel polynomials satisfies the system of delay-differential equations (Theorem
6.15
)

\begin{split}&f_{t}(x+1,\,t)-2f_{t}(x,\,t)+xf(x-1,\,t)=0,\\
&(1-2t)f_{t}(x,t)+(x-1)f(x,t)-2xf(x-1,t)=0.\end{split}

(1.27)

This paper is organised as follows. Section
2
explains some examples of

D
-modules structures on certain spaces of analytic functions and spaces of sequences of analytic functions
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, as well as
D
-linear maps between them. A list summarising these entities is given in Appendix
C.3
. The remaining parts of the §
2
gives a rudiment of holonomic
D
-modules theory needed in this article. A kind of generalised Borel resummation is also introduced. §
3
introduces the Bessel modules
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
for arbitrary
\nu
and the method of characteristic in solving a system of PDEs. Before discussing about the Bessel modules, we also discuss analogues of “elementary functions” such as the Weyl-exponentials, Weyl-trigonometry, and Weyl-Bessel.
This section also contains the statements and proofs of generating functions for the classical Bessel functions and difference Bessel functions, plus some immediate applications of these results to yield difference analogue of the classical Schläfli-Sonine integral representation from classical Bessel functions to difference Bessel functions. In section §
4
we modify the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{1/2}
to the “
reverse Bessel module
”
\Theta
by the introducing an integrating factor. We work out a characteristic for
\Theta
as well as a variant
\mathcal{Y}
corresponding to the usual Bessel polynomials
y_{n}
. These characteristics will be applied to find (Poisson type) generating functions in due course. As applications of the
D
-modules approach, we derive, in a
uniform way
, the well-known three-term recursion formulae for Bessel polynomials as well as the corresponding ones for the difference reverse Bessel polynomials. Section §
5
introduces the
Newton transform
,
a kind of generalised Mellin transform but with very different purpose as a
D
-linear map from
\mathcal{O}_{d}
to
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, namely the
D
-modules of analytic functions endowed with a forward difference operator. The Newton transformation serves as an effective tool to obtain generating functions for difference Bessel functions. Utilising results obtained from §
4
, we
give detailed proofs of generating functions for the reverse Bessel polynomials as well as their difference analogues in §
6.2
. Section §
7
take the half-Bessel modules from §
4
a step further to study trigonometric-type generating functions for the Bessel functions first obtained by Glaisher
[
64
]
. Before the concluding section §
9
, §
8

discusses our main results against previous results of Truesdell amongst others with some historical notes. In particular, it focus the philosophy that Truesdell pursued, and similarly of Wilf and Zeilberger, to find general methods of discovery of what kinds of formulae should exist rather than by ad hoc manipulations of formulae, a viewpoint shared by this study.
Appendix
A
contains proofs of various Theorems and Propositions stated but not fully verified in all aspects in the main text. Appendix
B
extends the study of the manifestation of the Bessel modules
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
to
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta_{h}}
, that is finite difference with arbitrary step size
h
. In the case of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
, the generating function derived unifies the case when
h=1
for the difference Bessel functions and the classical Bessel functions when
h\to 0
. This presents a further unification of the generating functions of the classical Bessel functions and that of difference Bessel functions of arbitrary step sizes. Appendix §
C
gives lists of
D
-modules used, transmutation formulae, the systems of PDEs encountered for various generating functions defined in their appropriate analytic functions space of two variables.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Linear maps between
D
-modules

Definition 2.1

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}.
The Weyl-algebra
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra with
2n
generators

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}

subject to the relations

[X_{i},X_{j}]=0;\hskip 10.00002pt[\partial_{i},\partial_{j}]=0;\hskip 10.00002pt[\partial_{i},X_{j}]=\delta_{ij}.

In this paper, we denote
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathbb{C}\langle X,\partial\rangle
or by
\mathcal{A}
when this is clear from the context.

We gather here preliminary material about
D
-module that we shall employ to exhibit a unified theory for the classical Bessel functions and generating functions for the recently discovered difference Bessel functions by Bohner and Cuchta
[
4
]
in the next sections. More specifically, amongst the generating functions studied include

•

extensions of generating functions of various kinds for the classical Bessel functions of arbitrary order
\nu
;

•

new generating functions for difference Bessel functions
[
4
]
;

•

new generating functions of Glaisher (Poisson)-type for difference Bessel functions;

•

new generating functions for new difference Bessel polynomials.

The primary concern of this paper is on
{D}
-linear maps defined on the Bessel modules and their induced generating functions to be introduced in the following two sections. Hence some generating functions found for difference Bessel functions may not converge in the same way as those of their classical counterparts.

The principle of obtaining these generating functions behind is based on the finite dimensionality of the space of
D
-linear maps from
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
to different
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-modules. The first step is the introduction of some examples of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-modules and linear maps between them which are frequently used in this article.

Definition 2.2
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}(1/X)
the Weyl-algebra
\mathbb{C}\langle\partial,X\rangle(1/X)=\mathbb{C}\langle\partial,X,{1}/{X}\rangle
modulo the aforementioned relations.

Example 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the space of analytic functions on a certain domain. Then the
\mathcal{O}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}
-module denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{d}
, if one endows it with the structure

\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial f)(t)=f^{\prime}(t),\\
(Xf)(t)=tf(t)\quad\mbox{for all }f\in\mathcal{O}\mbox{ and }t\mbox{ in this domain}.\end{array}

Similarly, let
\mathcal{O}_{2}
be the space of analytic functions on a certain domain in
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}
. Then the
\mathcal{O}_{2}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
, if one endows it with the structure

\begin{split}(\partial_{1}f)(x,\,t)&=f_{x}(x,\,t),\quad(X_{1}f)(x,\,t)=xf(x,\,t),\\
(\partial_{2}f)(x,\,t)&=f_{t}(x,\,t),\quad(X_{2}f)(x,\,t)=tf(x,\,t).\end{split}

(2.1)

Example 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the space of analytic functions. Then the
\mathcal{O}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}
-module, denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, if one endows it
with the structure

\begin{split}&(\partial f)(t)=f(t+1)-f(t),\\
&(Xf)(t)=tf(t-1).\end{split}

Example 2.5
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the space of all functions
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{C}
(bilateral sequences). Let

a=(a_{n})=(\cdots,a_{-2},\,a_{-1},\,a_{0},\,a_{1},\,a_{2},\,\cdots)

denote a bilateral sequence where
a_{k}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then the
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}
-module by defining

\begin{array}[]{l}(\partial a)_{n}=(n+1)a_{n+1}\\
(Xa)_{n}=a_{n-1}\quad\mbox{ for all bilateral sequences }(a_{n}).\end{array}

Various growth conditions can be imposed to such bilateral sequences.

2.1.1.
z
-transform I

Theorem 2.6

(
(Generating function of numeric sequences)
)
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
consists only of sequences
\{a_{n}\}
satisfying that

0\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}{|a_{-n}|^{1/n}}<\frac{1}{\limsup_{n\to\infty}{|a_{n}|^{1/n}}}\leq+\infty,

and let
\mathcal{O}
be the space of analytic functions defined on a certain annulus centred at
0
. Then the z-transform

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{z}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{d},\\
(a_{n})&\longmapsto&\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}t^{n}.\end{array}

is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear.

Proof.

The fact that the map
\mathfrak{z}
is
\mathbb{C}
-linear is obvious. The verification that both

\mathfrak{z}\,\partial(a_{n})=\partial\,\mathfrak{z}(a_{n}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{z}\,X(a_{n})=X\,\mathfrak{z}(a_{n})

hold are routine, with the understanding that the maps
\partial
and
X
are interpreted appropriately in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{O}_{d}
respectively. Hence the map
\mathfrak{z}
is
\mathcal{A}
-linear. Clearly, it is both injective and surjective.
∎

In fact, Theorem
2.6
can be extended to Theorem
2.6
′
below, which handles a larger class of sequences
\{a_{n}\}
so that
\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}t^{n}
may not converge.

Definition 2.7

(
(Borel resummation)
)
.

Suppose that
\{a_{n}\}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies both

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{|a_{n}|^{1/n}}<+\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\left|\frac{a_{-n}}{n!}\right|^{1/n}}<+\infty.

(2.2)

Then the
Borel resummation
of the Laurent series
\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}{a_{n}t^{n}}
is defined by taking the Laplace transform of the Borel transform of its principal part, i.e.,

\int_{0}^{+\infty}{e^{-st}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\frac{a_{-n-1}}{n!}}s^{n}\right)\,ds}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{a_{n}t^{n}}.

(2.3)

Remark 2.8
.

A power series or Laurent series whose sequence of coefficients
\{a_{n}\}
satisfy that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\left|\frac{a_{n}}{n!}\right|^{1/n}}<+\infty

is said to be
1
-Gevrey
. Note that the Borel resummation of a
1
-Gevrey Laurent series always exists as an analytic function, and the Borel resummation of a convergent Laurent series is just its ordinary sum, which is again an analytic function. We refer the reader to
[
49
, Part II]
,
[
59
]
and
[
19
]
for general discussions of
1
-Gevrey or
m
-Gevrey series and their Borel-resummation.

2.1.2.
z
-transform II

Theorem
2.6
′

(
(Generating function of numeric sequences)
)
.

Suppose that
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
consists only of sequences
\{a_{n}\}
satisfying both

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{|a_{n}|^{1/n}}<+\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\left|\frac{a_{-n}}{n!}\right|^{1/n}}<+\infty,

and let
\mathcal{O}
be the space of analytic functions defined on a certain region. Then the z-transform

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{z}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{d},\\
(a_{n})&\longmapsto&\mathfrak{B}\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}t^{n},\end{array}

where the
\mathfrak{B}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}t^{n}
denotes the Borel-resummation of
\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}a_{n}t^{n}
, is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear.

Proof.

Since the Borel and Laplace transforms are both left
\mathcal{A}
-linear, the Borel resummation is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear also.
∎

Example 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{2}
be a space of analytic functions in two variables. Then
\mathcal{O}_{2}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module by

\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{1}f(x,\,t)=f(x+1,\,t)-f(x,\,t),&X_{1}f(x,\,t)=xf(x-1,\,t);\\
\partial_{2}f(x,\,t)=f_{t}(x,\,t),&X_{2}f(x,\,t)=tf(x,\,t).\end{array}

(2.4)

Such a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module is denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
.

Remark 2.10
.

In addition to the above notation
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
, we shall liberally adopt notations such as
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
,
\mathcal{O}_{d\Delta}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta\Delta}
to represent different left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structures endowed on
\mathcal{O}_{2}
with respect to the corresponding operators.

We next extend the idea of space of bilateral sequences of numbers
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
in Example
2.5
to the space of bilateral sequences of analytic functions
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Example 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the space of all bilateral sequences of analytic functions with appropriately imposed growth restriction. Let

(f_{n})=(\cdots,\,f_{-1},\,f_{0},\,f_{1},\,\cdots)

denote a bilateral infinite sequence of analytic functions. Then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module by

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{1}f)_{n}(x)=f^{\prime}_{n}(x),&(X_{1}f)_{n}(x)=xf_{n}(x)\\
(\partial_{2}f)_{n}(x)=(n+1)f_{n+1}(x),&(X_{2}f)_{n}(x)=f_{n-1}(x),\end{array}

for all
n
and for all
x
. This left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module is denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Example 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the space of all bilateral sequences of analytic functions with an appropriately imposed growth restriction. Let

(f_{n})=(\cdots,\,f_{-1},\,f_{0},\,f_{1},\,\cdots)

denote a bilateral infinite sequence of
f_{k}\in\mathcal{O}
. Then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module, by

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{1}f)_{n}(x)=\Delta f_{n}(x)=f_{n}(x+1)-f_{n}(x),&(X_{1}f)_{n}(x)=xf_{n}(x-1)\\
(\partial_{2}f)_{n}(x)=(n+1)f_{n+1}(x),&(X_{2}f)_{n}(x)=f_{n-1}(x),\end{array}

(2.5)

for all bilateral sequences of analytic functions
(f_{n})
. This left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module is denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Similar to Theorem
2.6
, we have the following:

Theorem 2.13

(
(Generating function of sequences in
\mathcal{O}_{d}
)
)
.

The following z-transform

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{z}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{d}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{dd},\\
(f_{n})&\longmapsto&\mathfrak{B}\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)\,t^{n}\end{array}

(2.6)

is left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear.

Theorem 2.14

(
(Generating function of sequences in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
)
)
.

The following z-transform

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{z}_{\triangle}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\Delta}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d},\\
(f_{n})&\longmapsto&\mathfrak{B}\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}f_{n}(x)\,t^{n}\end{array}

(2.7)

is left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear.

One needs to make a slight modification of defining rules for
\mathcal{A}
-module in order to study one-sided (ordinary) sequences. The motivation of this rule will be explained in Remark
6.3
after we introduce the Poisson transform.

Example 2.15
.

Let
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
be the space of sequences

a=(a_{n})=(a_{1},\,a_{2},\,a_{3},\,\cdots)

of complex numbers. It is equipped with the structure of a left
\mathcal{A}
-module by defining

\begin{array}[]{l}(Xa)_{n}=na_{n-1}\\
(\partial a)_{n}=a_{n+1}\quad\mbox{for all sequences }(a_{n}).\end{array}

Similarly, let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the space of all sequences of analytic functions with appropriately imposed growth restriction. Let

(f_{n})=(f_{0},\,f_{1},\,f_{2},\,\cdots)

denote an infinite sequence of
f_{k}\in\mathcal{O}
. Then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module by

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{1}f)_{n}(x)=f^{\prime}_{n}(x),&(X_{1}f)_{n}(x)=xf_{n}(x)\\
(\partial_{2}f)_{n}(x)=f_{n+1}(x),&(X_{2}f)_{n}(x)=nf_{n-1}(x),\end{array}

(2.8)

for all sequences of analytic functions
(f_{n})
. This left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module is usually denoted as
\mathcal{O}_{d}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

Example 2.16
.

Let
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be the space of all sequences of analytic functions with appropriately imposed growth restriction. Let

(f_{n})=(f_{0},\,f_{1},\,f_{2},\,\cdots)

denote an infinite sequence of
f_{k}\in\mathcal{O}
. Then
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module by

\begin{array}[]{ll}(\partial_{1}f)_{n}(x)=f_{n}(x+1)-f_{n}(x),&(X_{1}f)_{n}(x)=xf_{n}(x-1)\\
(\partial_{2}f)_{n}(x)=f_{n+1}(x),&(X_{2}f)_{n}(x)=nf_{n-1}(x),\end{array}

(2.9)

for all sequences of analytic functions
(f_{n})
. This left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module is usually denoted as
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

We first demonstrate how we use the theory set up so far to derive a classical generating function for Bessel functions from the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
defined in Definition
3.38
which is isomorphic to Definition

3.15
.

A table summarising all the
D
-module structures relevant to this article is included in the Appendix
C.1
.

2.2.
Integrability criteria

This section deals with the idea of holonomic modules, which can be found in
[
17
]
. A quick revision is included here so that this article is self-contained.
For each multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
X_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots X_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}
will be abbreviated as
X^{\alpha}
. A similar abbreviation is adopted for the
\partial
’s. It is also a standard notation that
|\alpha|=|(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})|=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}
.

Definition 2.17
.

The
Bernstein filtration
of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is the filtration
F^{0}\mathcal{A}_{n}\subset F^{1}\mathcal{A}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{A}_{n}
defined by

F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}=\mbox{the }\mathbb{C}\mbox{-vector space spanned by }\{X^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}:|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq k\}.

Remark 2.18
.

The Bernstein filtration enjoys the following properties.

•

F^{i}\mathcal{A}_{n}\cdot F^{j}\mathcal{A}_{n}\subset F^{i+j}\mathcal{A}_{n}
,

•

[F^{i}\mathcal{A}_{n},F^{j}\mathcal{A}_{n}]\subset F^{i+j-1}\mathcal{A}_{n}
,

•

the
k^{\footnotesize\mbox{th}}-
graded piece
\mathrm{Gr}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}=F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}/F^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{n}
has finite
\mathbb{C}
-dimension for all
k
.

In fact, in the following treatment, the Bernstein filtration can be replaced by any other filtration having the these properties.

Lemma 2.19
.

The graded ring of the Bernstein filtration is a commutative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. Indeed,

\mathrm{Gr}\,\mathcal{A}_{n}=\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{Gr}^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}\cong\mathbb{C}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n},\partial_{1},\ldots,\partial_{n}].

Definition 2.20
.

Let
M
be a left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module. A
good filtration
of
M
is a filtration
\Gamma
which satisfies

•

F^{i}\mathcal{A}_{n}\cdot\Gamma^{j}M\subset\Gamma^{i+j}M
,

•

there exists
J\in\mathbb{N}
such that

F^{i}\mathcal{A}_{n}\cdot\Gamma^{j}M=\Gamma^{i+j}M
for all
i
,
j>J
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
M
be a finitely generated left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module and
\Gamma
be a good filtration of
M
. Then the graded ring of
\Gamma
, that is,

\mathrm{Gr}\,M=\bigoplus_{k}{\Gamma^{k}M/\Gamma^{k+1}M},

is a commutative algebra over the commutative ring
\mathrm{Gr}\,\mathcal{A}_{n}
.

Theorem 2.22

((Hilbert))
.

Let
M
be a finitely generated left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module equipped with a good filtration
\Gamma
. Then there exists a polynomial
P
such that

P(k)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma^{k}M\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for every sufficiently large }k.

Definition 2.23
.

The polynomial
P
in the last theorem is called the
Hilbert polynomial
of
M
. (It turns out that
P
is independent of the choice of the good filtration chosen.). If
P
is of degree
d
and its leading coefficient is
m/d!
, then we call
d
the
dimension
of
M
and
m
the
multiplicity
of
M
.

Example 2.24
.

Consider
\mathcal{A}_{n}
as a left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module and the Bernstein filtration is a good one. Then,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}=\binom{k+2n}{2n}

so that the dimension of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is
2n
and its multiplicity is
1
.

Example 2.25
.

Consider

M=\mathcal{A}_{n}/\mathcal{A}_{n}\partial_{1}+\cdots+\mathcal{A}_{n}\partial_{n},

which inherits a good filtration
\Gamma
from the Bernstein filtration. Then,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma^{k}M=\binom{k+n}{n}

so that the dimension of
M
is
n
and its multiplicity is
1
.

Example 2.26
.

Consider

M=\mathcal{A}_{2}/\mathcal{A}_{2}\partial_{1}^{2}+\mathcal{A}_{2}\partial_{2},

which inherits a good filtration
\Gamma
from the Bernstein filtration. Then,

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Gamma^{k}M=(k+1)^{2}

so that the dimension of
M
and its multiplicity are both equal to
2
.

Theorem 2.27

((
Bernstein inequality
))
.

For each non-trivial finitely generated left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module
M
, its dimension
d(M)
satisfies

n\leq d(M)\leq 2n.

Definition 2.28
.

A finitely generated left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module is
holonomic
if it is nontrivial and its dimension is
n
.

Example 2.29
.

\mathcal{A}_{n}
is not a holonomic left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module. However,
M=\mathcal{A}_{n}/(\mathcal{A}_{n}\partial_{1}+\cdots+\mathcal{A}_{n}\partial_{n})
is a holonomic left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-module.

Example 2.30

((
Integrability
))
.

Let
p(X_{1},X_{2})
,
q(X_{1},X_{2})\in\mathbb{C}[X_{1},X_{2}]
be polynomials in two variables. Denote their formal partial derivatives by subscripts. Consider the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module

M=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\partial_{1}+p(X_{1},X_{2}))+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\partial_{2}+q(X_{1},X_{2}))}.

•

If
p_{2}(X_{1},X_{2})\neq q_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})
, then
M

is trivial and is therefore not holonomic.

•

If
p_{2}(X_{1},X_{2})=q_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})
, then
M
can be analyzed in a way similar to the previous examples, and it turns out that
M
is holonomic with its (Hilbert) dimension two and multiplicity one in this case as described in Example
2.25
.

The condition
p_{2}(X_{1},X_{2})=q_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})
is known as an
integrability condition
. Integrability conditions give rise to holonomic
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-modules, which are important because their “solution space” (see below) has finite
\mathbb{C}
-dimension. Such a
D
-module thus ensembles the basic features of an ODE. The examples of
D
-modules which we study in this article are going to be holonomic.

2.3.
Banach algebra and Poincaré-Perron theory

In order to study the convergence issue of Bessel’s generating functions that are results of the
z
-transforms introduced in the last subsection, we extend the classical Poincaré-Perron theory from sequences of complex numbers to sequences in Banach algebra
[
69
]
. In our applications we only need to consider sequences in Banach algebra satisfying second order linear difference equations. However, our adaption can clearly be extended to higher order difference equations.

Theorem 2.31

((
Poincaré-Perron theorem in Banach algebras)).

Let (
\mathbb{B},\|\cdot\|
) be a Banach algebra. Suppose that the
a
,
b\in\mathbb{B}
and
(p_{n})
,
(q_{n})
are sequences in
\mathbb{B}
converging to zero as
n\to\infty
. If the characteristic equation of the associated difference equation

y_{n+2}+ay_{n+1}+by_{n}=0,

(2.10)

of the difference equation

y_{n+2}+(a+p_{n})y_{n+1}+(b+q_{n})y_{n}=0,

(2.11)

has roots
\lambda_{1},\,\lambda_{2}
distinct in norms, then for each solution
y_{n}
to (
2.11
), the limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\|y_{n+1}\|}{\|y_{n}\|}=\|\lambda_{j}\|,

holds, for some
j=1,\,2
.

Proofs of Poincaré’s original statement can be found from either Gel’fond
[
29
, §5.5.2]
or Milne-Thomson
[
48
, XVII]
.
The proof of the above theorem is a straightforward adaptation in the
proof of Poincaré’s original statement with
\mathbb{B}
in place of
\mathbb{C}
.
Since the proof of Poincaré’s theorem in full generality is long and the its adaption in Banach algebra
\mathbb{B}
is almost verbatim except for the aforementioned replacement of “
|\cdot|
” by “
\|\cdot\|
” in all the computation and estimates,
one can verify that a shorter proof of Poincaré’s theorem available for third order linear difference equations
discussed in
[
48
]
can easily be adopted to the Banach algebra
\mathbb{B}
setting.
Indeed, there are different adaptations of Poincaré-Perron theory in the literature. For example, the author in
[
57
]
considered objects that assume values in certain abelian groups in place of
complex numbers. Although the asymptotic of the classical Bessel function
J_{\nu+n}
as
n\to\infty
is well-known, a corresponding result for the difference Bessel function
J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}
is unknown and appears highly non-trivial. The adaption of Poincaré’s theorem in a Banach algebra setting can easily produce the needed, though rough, asymptotic for the generating function of difference Bessel functions in to be stated in Theorem
3.39
.

3.
Bessel modules and Bessel functions

In this section, we investigate the generating functions of the Bessel functions. We will construct an important holonomic left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module, called the Bessel module, from which we obtain a pair of PDEs that the generating function satisfy.

Definition 3.1

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let
M,N
be left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-modules. A
solution
of
M
in
N
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{n}
-linear maps from
M
to
N
. In particular, the set of
all solutions
of
M
in
N
is denoted by

\mathop{\rm Hom}(M,N).

Theorem 3.2

(
[
13
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\langle X\partial,X\rangle
be the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
generated by
X\partial
and
X
. Then there exists an element
S=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}X^{k}
in the
X
-adic completion
\mathbb{C}[[X]]
so that the map

\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial-\lambda)

is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear, i.e., it gives a solution of
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L
in
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial-\lambda)
.

The element
S
in the above theorem is a Weyl-algebraic analogue of a
Frobenius series expansion
of a solution of
L
.
In fact, each left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}K
induces a map between solution sets

\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}K,N)\longrightarrow\mathop{\rm Hom}(\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L,N).

Thus finding solutions of a differential operator
L
in terms of solutions of another differential operator
K
is equivalent to identifying an
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}K.

For instance, finding “power series solutions” of a differential operator
L
is equivalent to a left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L\to\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial
; finding a “Frobenius series solution” of
L
is equivalent to a left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L\to\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(X\partial-\nu)
; and so on. We refer to
[
13
]
for the details. We illustrate the above discussion with the following examples that will be needed for the rest of this paper.

3.1.
Weyl series solutions

Example 3.3

((
Weyl-exponential
))
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
. A solution of
L:=\partial-a
, i.e. a solution of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)
in any other
D
-module, is called a
Weyl-exponential
. In this example, we consider solutions of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)
in
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial
, that is, we look for solutions as “power series” of
X
, which are called
Weyl power series
to the Weyl exponential. For this purpose we compute the element
S=\sum_{k}c_{k}X^{k}
which gives rise to a
left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial}

guaranteed by the study in
[
13
, §4.1]
. For each
k\geq 1
, since
\partial X^{k}=X^{k}\partial+kX^{k-1}
, we have
that

\begin{split}0=(\partial-a)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}\right)&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}\partial+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}kX^{k-1}-a\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(c_{k+1}(k+1)-ac_{k})X^{k}.\quad\qquad\mod\mathcal{A}\partial\end{split}

The above formula gives recurrence of
c_{k}
from the relation

kc_{k}-ac_{k-1}=0,\hskip 20.00003ptk\geq 1.

By choosing
c_{0}=1,
we obtain
c_{k}=a^{k}/k!
. Thus we denote

S(X)={\mathop{\rm E}}(aX):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{k}}{k!}X^{k}

(3.1)

to be the
Weyl exponential series
.

Example 3.4
.

(
Weyl Sine and Cosine
) The
Weyl-sine
and the
Weyl-cosine
are solutions of
L:=\partial^{2}+1
, i.e., solutions of the
D
-module
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)
. To express them as “Weyl power series”, i.e., in terms of powers of
X
, we again let
K=\partial
, and compute the element
S=\sum c_{k}X^{k}
which yields the left
\mathcal{A}

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial},

(3.2)

which are solutions of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)
in
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial
.
Since
\partial^{2}+1=(\partial-i)(\partial+i)
, we apply Example
3.3
with the choice
a=-i
from the Example
3.3
. Then it follows immediately that

(\partial-i)(\partial+i){\mathop{\rm E}}(-iX)=0\hskip 20.00003pt\mod\partial

and since
\partial^{2}+1=(\partial+i)(\partial-i)
so that

(\partial+i)(\partial-i){\mathop{\rm E}}(iX)=0\hskip 20.00003pt\mod\partial,

where we have chosen
a=i
from the Example
3.3
. Thus both
{\mathop{\rm E}}(iX)
and
{\mathop{\rm E}}(-iX)
in
\mathcal{A}
are possible choices of
S
in (
3.2
). Hence both

\mathop{\rm Sin}(X):=\frac{1}{2i}\big({\mathop{\rm E}}(iX)-{\mathop{\rm E}}(-iX)\big)

(3.3)

and

\mathop{\rm Cos}(X):=\frac{1}{2}\big({\mathop{\rm E}}(iX)+{\mathop{\rm E}}(-iX)\big)

(3.4)

are different choices of elements of
\mathcal{A}
, called the
Weyl-Sine and Cosine series
, that can also serve for the
S
in (
3.2
).
corresponding

Alternatively, we may compute the Weyl Sine and Cosine series
S=\sum c_{k}X^{k}

directly
by

\begin{split}0&=(\partial-i)(\partial+i)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}\partial^{2}+2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}kX^{k-1}\partial+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}c_{k}k(k-1)X^{k-2}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k+2}(k+1)(k+2)X^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}.\qquad\mod\partial\end{split}

That is,

c_{k+2}=\frac{-c_{k}}{(k+1)(k+2)},\hskip 10.00002ptk\geq 0.

Thus, if
c_{0}=0,c_{1}=1
, we obtain Weyl Sine series

\mathop{\rm Sin}X=X-\frac{X^{3}}{3!}+\frac{X^{5}}{5!}-\cdots+\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(2k-1)!}X^{2k-1}+\cdots.

If
c_{0}=1,c_{1}=0
, we obtain Weyl (power) Cosine series

\mathop{\rm Cos}X=1-\frac{X^{2}}{2!}+\frac{X^{4}}{4!}-\cdots+\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2k)!}X^{2k}+\cdots.

Hence they serve as
S
with “appropriately imposed initial conditions”.

We now introduce one of the main subjects of study in this paper.

Example 3.5

((
Weyl Bessel of order
\nu
))
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
such that
2\nu
is not an integer, and let

L=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}.

(3.5)

Let
\mathcal{D}=\langle X\partial,X\rangle
be the subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
generated by
X\partial
and
X
. Then the
Weyl Bessel of order
\nu
is a solution of
L
in a left
\mathcal{D}
-module
N
, i.e., a solution of
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L
in
N
. To find the “Frobenius series expansion” of this Weyl Bessel, we look for a solution of
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L
in the
D
-module
\overline{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial-\nu)}
, i.e., we find the element
S=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}
that gives the left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial-\nu)}.

(3.6)

Since

(X\partial+\nu)(X\partial-\nu)X^{k}=X^{k}[(X\partial+\nu)+2k](X\partial-\nu)+(2\nu k+k^{2})X^{k},

holds for every
k\geq 0
(as well as trivially when
k=0
), we have

\begin{split}0&=L\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}[(X\partial+\nu)(X\partial-\nu)+X^{2}]X^{k}\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k}[(X\partial+\nu)+2k](X\partial-\nu)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{c_{k}k(2\nu+k)X^{k}}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k+2}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}k(2\nu+k)X^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{k}X^{k+2}\hfill\mod X\partial-\nu\\
&=c_{1}(1+2\nu)X+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}(c_{k}k(2\nu+k)+c_{k-2})X^{k},\hfill\mod X\partial-\nu\end{split}

which implies that
c_{1}=c_{3}=c_{5}=\cdots=0
, and by choosing
c_{0}\Gamma(\nu+1)=2^{-\nu},
we must have

c_{2k}=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)},\hskip 10.00002ptk\geq 0.

This shows that

S=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}X^{2k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)}

can be chosen as such a Weyl series
S
in (
3.6
) that serves as a solution for (
3.6
). We denote this specific Weyl Bessel series by
\mathcal{J}_{\nu}
as solution in
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial-\nu)
, or more generally, in a left
\mathcal{A}
-module
N
, which can be considered as the Weyl algebraic version of the classical Bessel
J_{\nu}
. We caution that the reader not to confuse the series form of
\mathcal{J}_{\nu}
in contrast to the original Bessel function of order
J_{\nu}
since there is no factor “
X^{\nu}
” that would appear there unless when the
\nu
reduces to an integer.

3.2.
Transmutation formulae

After defining the Weyl Bessel as a solution of
L=L_{\nu}:=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}
in the last subsection, we observe the following transmutation formulae for
L
. They can be verified directly, but they do not appear to be found immediately amongst the formulae in Dereziński and Majewski
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
14
14

14

Please see §
9
Conclusion for discussion related to the work of Infeld and Hull
[
35
]
.
. As we shall see that they are instrumental in the development of Bessel module to be defined in the next subsection.

Proposition 3.6
.

For each
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle[(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu-1)^{2}]\left(\partial+\dfrac{\nu}{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial+\dfrac{\nu-2}{X}\right)[(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}],

(3.7)

\displaystyle[(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu+1)^{2}]\left(\partial-\dfrac{\nu}{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial-\dfrac{\nu+2}{X}\right)[(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}],

(3.8)

which induce the following left
\mathcal{A}
-linear maps

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu-1)^{2})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\partial+\frac{\nu}{X})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}),\\
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu+1)^{2})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\partial-\frac{\nu}{X})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}).\end{array}

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
, the classical Bessel functions satisfy the following recurrence relations.

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{\nu}(x)+\dfrac{\nu J_{\nu}(x)}{x}

\displaystyle=J_{\nu-1}(x),

(3.9)

\displaystyle J^{\prime}_{\nu}(x)-\dfrac{\nu J_{\nu}(x)}{x}

\displaystyle=-J_{\nu+1}(x).

(3.10)

Proof.

This proof serves to provide an alternative to the classical proof found in most literature.
It is a direct consequence from the previous Proposition with the
D
-module structure on
\mathcal{O}
introduced from the Example
2.3
. Let
J_{\nu}
be the classical Bessel function to
L_{\nu}
with the asymptotic behaviour
\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}{J_{\nu}(x)}/{x^{\nu}}={1}/{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)}

[
64
, p. 43]
. Then one applies the both sides of (
3.7
). It implies that
(\partial+\nu/X)J_{\nu}=CJ_{\nu-1}+DJ_{-\nu+1}
holds for some constants
C,D
. One can easily verify that the
C=1,\ D=0
from the asymptotic
J^{\prime}_{\nu}(x)/x^{\nu-1}\sim 1/2^{\nu-1}\Gamma(\nu)

[
64
, p. 43]
. This verifies the (
3.9
). The verification of (
3.10
) is similar.

∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and a left
\mathcal{D}
-module
N
be given. Then there exists a sequence
\mathrm{(}\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
in
N^{\mathbb{Z}}
and for each
n
the
\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}
is a solution to
L_{n}=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu+n)^{2}
in
N
, such that we have

\displaystyle X\partial\,\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}+(\nu+n)\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}-X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n-1}=0,

(3.11)

\displaystyle X\partial\,\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}-(\nu+n)\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}+X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n+1}=0.

(3.12)

Remark 3.9
.

Reader are cautioned not to confuse the notation
\mathfrak{J}_{\nu}
introduced in the last Corollary with notation
\mathcal{J}_{\nu}
that denotes the Weyl-Bessel series introduced in Example
3.5
that is defined as a solution with respect to the (
3.6
).

Proof.

Recall that
L_{\nu}=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}
. Let us replace
\nu
by
\nu+n
in (
3.7
). Then it is clear that the right-side of (
3.7
) annihilates
\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}
. However the expression
(\partial+\nu/X)\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}=(X\partial+\nu)/X\,(X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu}
) on the left-side must be annihilated by
L_{\nu+n-1}
. Hence
X\partial\,\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}+(\nu+n)\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}=\pm X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n-1}
. This gives the (
3.11
) except for the “
\mp
” in front of the third term
X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n-1}
. We have chosen the minus sign over the plus sign in accordance with the corresponding formula for the classical Bessel function
J_{\nu}
. This establishes the (
3.11
). The proof for (
3.12
) is similar.
∎

Corollary 3.10

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and a left
\mathcal{D}
-module
N
be given. Then there exists a sequence
\mathrm{(}\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
such that for each
n
the
\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}
is a solution to
L_{n}=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu+n)^{2}
in
N
, such that we have

2(\nu+n)\,\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n}-X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n-1}-X\mathfrak{J}_{\nu+n+1}=0

(3.13)

as an element in
N
.

3.3.
Bessel modules

Recall that the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is the free
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathbb{C}\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{2},X_{1},X_{2}\rangle
subject to

\partial_{i}\partial_{j}-\partial_{j}\partial_{i}=0,\hskip 10.00002ptX_{i}X_{j}-X_{j}X_{i}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\partial_{i}X_{j}-X_{j}\partial_{i}=\delta_{ij},

for
i,j=1,2
. Then we define

Definition 3.11
.

We denote the algebra obtained by adjoining the new elements
1/X_{1},\,1/X_{2}
to the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2}
by

\mathcal{A}_{2}(1/X_{1},\,1/X_{2}):=\mathbb{C}\langle\partial_{1},\partial_{2},X_{1},\dfrac{1}{X_{1}},X_{2},\dfrac{1}{X_{2}}\rangle.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. Motivated by Corollary
3.8
, we now consider the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module generanted by the following elements

X_{1}\partial_{1}+(X_{2}\partial_{2}+\nu)-X_{1}X_{2},\hskip 20.00003ptX_{1}\partial_{1}-(X_{2}\partial_{2}+\nu)+\frac{X_{1}}{X_{2}}.

(3.14)

With the (
3.14
) in mind, we define

Definition 3.12

((
Bessel module of order
\nu
))
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. The left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module
15
15

15

The
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is in fact a quotient module. We have left out the word “quotient” when no confusion can arise.

\mathcal{B}_{\nu}=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}\partial_{1}+(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})-X_{1}X_{2})+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}\partial_{1}-(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})+{X_{1}}/{X_{2}})}

(3.15)

is called the
Bessel module of order

\nu
.

First we check that the
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is indeed holonomic.

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. The Bessel module

\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module

16
16

16

We omit the description that
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-quotient module for the sake of simplicity.

with dimension
2
and multiplicity
1
. In particular, it is holonomic.

Proof.

The two generators from Definition
3.12
that generate the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
satisfy the integrability criterion stated in Example
2.25
. Hence the Bessel module has dimension
2
and multiplicity
1
.
∎

The following theorem shows that one can recover from
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
the ODE satisfied by Bessel functions.

Proposition 3.14

((
Bessel ODE module of order
\nu
))
.

The map

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[(X_{1}\partial_{1})^{2}+X_{1}^{2}-(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})^{2}]}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{\nu}

is a well-defined left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear surjection.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is generated by the two elements

X_{1}\partial_{1}+X_{2}\partial_{2}-X_{1}X_{2}+\nu,\hskip 20.00003ptX_{1}\partial_{1}-X_{2}\partial_{2}+\frac{X_{1}}{X_{2}}-\nu

as in (
3.14
), so as an element in
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
, we have

\displaystyle(X_{1}\partial_{1})^{2}+X_{1}^{2}-(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=(X_{1}\partial_{1})^{2}+X_{1}^{2}+(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})(X_{1}\partial_{1}-X_{1}X_{2})

\displaystyle=(X_{1}\partial_{1})(X_{1}\partial_{1}+\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})+(X_{1}X_{2})(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{2}}-\nu-\partial_{2}X_{2})

\displaystyle=(X_{1}\partial_{1})(X_{1}X_{2})+(X_{1}X_{2})(X_{2}\partial_{2}-X_{1}\partial_{1}-\partial_{2}X_{2})

\displaystyle=(X_{1}\partial_{1})(X_{1}X_{2})+(X_{1}X_{2})(-\partial_{1}X_{1})

\displaystyle=0.

Hence the map is well defined.
∎

We may rephrase the above result in the following form.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and
M
be a left
\mathcal{A}
-module. If the sequence
(f_{n})
is a solution of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
M^{\mathbb{Z}}
, then for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f_{n}
is a solution of
(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-(\nu+n)^{2}
in
M
. In other words, the element
(X_{1}\partial_{1})^{2}+X_{1}^{2}-(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})^{2}
is equivalent to
0
in
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
.

3.4.
Characteristics of Bessel modules

We will see later that the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
gives rise to PDEs satisfied by the generating functions of the Bessel functions
(J_{\nu+n})
in §
3.5
as well as of the recently discovered difference Bessel functions
(J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n})
in §
3.6
below. The following theorem is an algebraic analogue of the
method of characteristics
, which will help in solving such PDEs.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
be defined above. Then there exists a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(1/X_{j=1,2})
-linear map
S

\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\xrightarrow{\times S}\overline{\mathcal{A}_{2}/\big[\mathcal{A}_{2}\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2}\partial_{2}+\nu)\big]}.

(3.16)

In fact, we can take

S={\mathop{\rm E}}\Big[\frac{X_{1}}{2}\Big(X_{2}-\frac{1}{X_{2}}\Big)\Big],

(3.17)

where
{\mathop{\rm E}}
denotes the Weyl exponential series introduced in Example
3.1
.

Proof.

Define new symbols
\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2},Y_{1},Y_{2}
by

Y_{1}=X_{1}X_{2},\hskip 20.00003ptY_{2}=\frac{X_{1}}{X_{2}},\hskip 20.00003pt2\Theta_{1}=\frac{\partial_{1}}{X_{2}}+\frac{\partial_{2}}{X_{1}},\hskip 20.00003pt2\Theta_{2}=X_{2}\partial_{1}-\frac{X^{2}_{2}\partial_{2}}{X_{1}}.

Direct verification yields

[\Theta_{1},Y_{1}]=1,\hskip 10.00002pt[\Theta_{2},Y_{2}]=1,\hskip 10.00002pt[\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2}]=0,\hskip 10.00002pt[Y_{1},Y_{2}]=0,\hskip 10.00002pt[\Theta_{i},\,Y_{j}]=0,\ i\not=j.

(3.18)

The elements in (
3.14
) become

2Y_{1}\Theta_{1}-Y_{1}+{\nu}\hskip 20.00003pt\mbox{and}\hskip 20.00003pt2Y_{2}\Theta_{2}+Y_{2}-{\nu}.

It follows from the Weyl exponential introduced in Example
3.3
for
a=\nu/2
that

\begin{split}(2Y_{1}\Theta_{1}-Y_{1}+\nu)\,{\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)&=2Y_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)\Theta_{1}+\nu{\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)\\
&={\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)(2Y_{1}\Theta_{1}+\nu)\\
&=0.\qquad\qquad\quad\mod\mathcal{A}_{2}(Y_{1}\Theta_{1}+\nu/2)\end{split}

In general we have
S=F(Y_{2})\cdot{\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)
for some factor
F(Y_{2})
depending on
Y_{2}
only. Next we require
F(Y_{2})
to satisfy that

(2Y_{2}\Theta_{2}+Y_{2}-\nu)\,F(Y_{2})\cdot{\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)=0.\hskip 20.00003pt\mod\mathcal{A}_{2}(Y_{2}\Theta_{2}-\nu/2)

That is,

(2Y_{2}\Theta_{2}+Y_{2}-\nu)\,F(Y_{2})=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\mod\mathcal{A}_{2}(Y_{2}\Theta_{2}-\nu/2).

It follows from a similar consideration as above that
F(Y_{2})=c{\mathop{\rm E}}(-Y_{2}/2)
for some complex number
c
.
Without loss of generality we may choose
c=1
so that the above map becomes

S:={\mathop{\rm E}}(Y_{1}/2)\cdot{\mathop{\rm E}}(-Y_{2}/2)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{X_{1}^{k}X_{2}^{k}}{2^{k}k!}\cdot\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}X_{1}^{\ell}}{X_{2}^{\ell}2^{\ell}\ell!}={\mathop{\rm E}}\Big[\frac{X_{1}}{2}\Big(X_{2}-\frac{1}{X_{2}}\Big)\Big]

(3.19)

which is the map asserted in (
3.16
).
∎

Remark 3.17
.

We may rewrite the product of two series in
S
in (
3.19
) to get

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{X^{k}_{1}X^{k}_{2}}{2^{k}k!}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{l}X^{\ell}_{1}}{X^{\ell}_{2}2^{\ell}!}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}X^{n+2\ell}_{1}}{2^{n+2\ell}(n+\ell)!\ell!}\right)X_{2}^{n}.

The “inner sum”

\mathcal{J}_{n}(X)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{(n+\ell)!l!}\left(\frac{X}{2}\right)^{n+2\ell}

agrees with Example
3.5
after composing with the multiplication by
X^{n}
from
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(X\partial-n)
to
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial
. It gives a “power series” of the Weyl Bessel of order
n
. Following the arguments used in Watson
[
64
, pp. 15-16]
that

\mathcal{J}_{-n}(X)=(-1)^{n}\mathcal{J}_{n}(X)

(3.20)

holds for each integer
n\geq 1
.

3.5.
Generating function of classical Bessel functions

As was explained by Watson
[
64
, Chap. 2., §2,.1]
that the convergence of the infinite expansion of the generating function

e^{\frac{x}{2}\big(t-\frac{1}{t}\big)}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{n}(x)\,t^{n}

(3.21)

of Bessel function is absolute in powers of
t
, and valid for all
x
and
t\not=0
. We shall take this expansion as the basis of our study of various extensions of this generating function for various types of Bessel functions in this section.

Example 3.18

((
Frobenius series of classical Bessel functions of order
\nu
))
.

Let
\nu
be a complex number such that
2\nu
is not an integer, and consider the the Bessel operator
L=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}
as in Example
3.5
. The classical Bessel function of order
\nu
,
J_{\nu}(x)
, is a solution of
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L
in the
D
-module
\mathcal{O}_{d}
. To obtain a Frobenius series expansion of
J_{\nu}(x)
, we
recall from Example
3.5
the element

S=\mathcal{J}_{\nu}(X)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}X^{2k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)}

(3.22)

gives the composition map
of left
\mathcal{A}
-linear maps

\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial-\nu)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times x^{\nu}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{d},

(3.23)

and so

J_{\nu}(x)=x^{\nu}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}x^{2k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)},

(3.24)

gives raise to the classical Bessel function of order
\nu
.

On the other hand, if we replace the
\nu
in (
3.12
) by
-\nu
, and proceed in a similar manner for an analogous set up, then we would have obtained a “linearly-independent” solution of
\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L

in
\mathcal{O}_{d}
given by

\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(X\partial+\nu)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times x^{-\nu}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{d},

(3.25)

where

S=\mathcal{J}_{-\nu}(X)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}X^{2k}}{2^{-\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(-\nu+k+1)}.

(3.26)

The (
3.25
) gives rise to the Frobenius series expansion

J_{-\nu}(x)=x^{-\nu}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}x^{2k}}{2^{-\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(-\nu+k+1)}.

(3.27)

The following example illustrates how sequences of analytic functions are realized from
D
-modules.

Example 3.19
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and consider the bilateral sequence of classical Bessel functions
(J_{\nu+n})_{n}
. Then the maps

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}_{+}:\mathcal{B}_{\nu}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(J_{\nu+n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}\end{array}

(3.28)

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}_{-}:\mathcal{B}_{\nu}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(J_{-\nu-n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}\end{array}

(3.29)

are both left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear.

More generally, a general solution of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
will be denoted by
\times\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
. That is,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}:\mathcal{B}_{\nu}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}\end{array}

(3.30)

The following proposition and corollary are direct consequences of Corollary
3.8
with
M=\mathcal{O}_{d}
and
\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}
.

Proposition 3.20

((
Differential-difference formulae
))
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\ \begin{subarray}{c}\times(\mathcal{C}_{\nu+n})\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}\ \mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{d}

\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
be a solution of
\mathcal{B_{\nu}}
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{d}
. Then the
\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}

satisfies the following classical differential-difference formulae

x\mathscr{C}_{\nu}^{\prime}(x)+\nu\mathscr{C}_{\nu}(x)-x\mathscr{C}_{\nu-1}(x)=0,

(3.31)

and

x\mathscr{C}^{\prime}_{\nu}(x)-\nu\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}(x)+x\mathscr{C}_{\nu+1}(x)=0.

(3.32)

The usage of the notation
\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}
follows Sonine’s notation (see
[
64
, p. 83]
) for functions that satisfy the system (
3.31
) and (
3.32
).

Here is an application of the proposition to recover two well-known formulae about the classical Bessel functions. We shall see how this will lead to new results for difference Bessel functions and half-Bessel modules for difference reverse Bessel polynomials in §
6.2
.

One recovers the well-known formulae (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) by choosing
\mathscr{C}_{\nu}=J_{\nu}
in the above Corollary.

The following classical three-term recursion formula is a consequence of the above differential-difference formulae (
3.31
) and (
3.32
).

Corollary 3.21

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\ \begin{subarray}{c}\times(\mathcal{C}_{\nu+n})\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}\ \mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{d}

\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
be a solution of
\mathcal{B_{\nu}}
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{d}
. Then

2\nu\mathscr{C}_{\nu}(x)-x\mathscr{C}_{\nu-1}(x)-x\mathscr{C}_{\nu+1}(x)=0.

(3.33)

Now we come to the main result of this subsection, which is about the generating function of the classical Bessel functions
(J_{\nu+n}(x))
. It is a two-variable analytic solution of the holonomic system of PDEs obtained from the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
(
3.15
).

Theorem 3.22

((
Asymptotic behaviour)).

The sequence
(J_{n+\nu})
is a uniformly 1-Gevrey compacta solution of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}_{d}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

It follows from the the Corollary
3.21
that
J_{\nu+n}
satisfies

y_{n+2}-\dfrac{2(n+\nu+1)}{x}y_{n+1}+y_{n}=0.

As a result, the modified sequence (
Y_{n}
) where
Y_{n}:=J_{\nu+n}/n!
satisfies the equation

Y_{n+2}-\dfrac{2(n+\nu+1)}{(n+2)x}Y_{n+1}+\dfrac{1}{(n+2)(n+1)}Y_{n}=0.

(3.34)

Now one fixes a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{C}\backslash\{0\}
. The space of functions analytic on
K
becomes a Banach algebra
\mathbb{B}_{K}
equipped with the usual sup norm
\|\cdot\|_{K}
.Therefore, we may apply the generalized Poincaré-Perron theory stated in Theorem
2.31
on the Banach algebra
\mathbb{B}_{K}
for the asymptotic behaviour of (
J_{\nu+n}
) on the compact set
K
. Since the characteristic equation of (
3.34
) is given by

\lambda^{2}-2\lambda/x=0,

so that its roots are
0
and
2/x
whose norms are obviously different.
Consequently

\|J_{n+\nu}/n!\|_{K}=O((2/d(K,0))^{n}).

(3.35)

That is, according to the Remark
2.8
, the sequence
(J_{n+\nu})
is
1
-Gevrey.
∎

Remark 3.23
.

(1)

It is instructive to compare the estimate (
3.35
)
with the well-known asymptotic

J_{\nu+n}(x)\sim\frac{e^{-\nu}}{\sqrt{2\pi n}}\Big(\frac{ex}{2n}\Big)^{\nu+n}

as given, see for example, in
[
28
, §5]
. An application of Stirling formula to (
3.35
) shows that we have the same order of growths from the two asymptotic estimates.
17
17

17

In fact, this can be seen from the elementary growth estimate
[
64
, p. 225]

J_{\nu}(x)\approx\exp\big\{\nu+\nu\log(x/2)-(\nu+\frac{1}{2})\log\nu\big\}\cdot\big[1/\sqrt{2\pi}+\frac{c_{1}}{\nu}+\frac{c_{2}}{\nu^{2}}+\cdots\big]

holds for a fixed
x
as
\nu\to\infty
,
so that although the “analystic part”, i.e., the “
\sum_{0}^{\infty}
” part of (
3.36
) is convergent, while the “principle part”, or the “
\sum_{-1}^{-\infty}
” is divergent.

(2)

Similarly, one sees that the sequence
(J_{-n+\nu})
is “uniformly 1-Gevrey compacta” also. The conclusion remains valid if the standard Bessel functions are replaced by an arbitrary sequence
(\mathscr{C}_{n+\nu})
as formulated above.

(3)

It follows from the last remark item that the
z
-transform of the sequence
(J_{n+\nu})
, or the Borel resummation of
\sum_{n}J_{n+\nu}t^{n}
, is a well-defined analytic functions in two variables as defined in the Definition
2.7
.

(4)

We also note that the conclusion of Theorem
3.22
remains valid if the uniform compacta norm is replaced by any other norm.

Theorem 3.24

((
Generating function of Bessel functions
))
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

Let
(\mathscr{C}_{n+\nu})_{n}
be a bilateral sequence of analytic functions which is a solution of the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then there exists a complex number
C_{\nu}
such that

C_{\nu}\,t^{-\nu}\exp\Big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t}\big)\Big]\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n},

(3.36)

where the symbol “
\sim
’’ above means that the left-side is the Borel resummation
18
18

18

Borel resummation is defined in Definition
2.7
.
of the right-hand side.

Moreover, the holonomic system of PDEs (
3.14
) when realized in

\mathcal{O}_{dd}
in Example
2.3
defined by (
2.1
) is given by

y_{x}+(1/t-t)/2\,y=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\nu y+ty_{t}-{x}/2\,(1/t+t)\,y=0.

(3.37)

To prove Theorem
3.24
, we need the following two lemmas, which serve the purpose of simplifying and solving the holonomic system of PDEs (
3.14
) in
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
.

Lemma 3.25
.

The Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is isomorphic to

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[\partial_{1}+\frac{1}{2}({1}/{X_{2}}-X_{2})]+\mathcal{A}_{2}[(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})-\frac{1}{2}X_{1}(1/X_{2}+X_{2})]}.

(3.38)

Lemma 3.26
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[\partial_{1}+\frac{1}{2}({1}/{X_{2}}-X_{2})]+\mathcal{A}_{2}[(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})-\frac{1}{2}X_{1}(1/X_{2}+X_{2})]}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times t^{-\nu}\exp[\frac{x}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})]}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}_{dd}\end{array}

is well-defined and left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear. In fact, this linear map is unique up to a complex multiple.

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify that the function
t^{-\nu}\exp[\frac{x}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})]
satisfies the system of PDEs

\frac{\partial y}{\partial x}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\dfrac{1}{t}-t\big)y=0,\hskip 20.00003pt\nu y+t\frac{\partial y}{\partial t}-\frac{x}{2}\big(\dfrac{1}{t}+t\big)y=0.

(3.39)

To see the uniqueness, for a general analytic function
y=y(x,\,t)
. The first equation yields
y(x,t)=g(t)\exp\big[(\dfrac{x}{2}(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]
for some
g
. Substituting this
y
into the second differential equation yields
g(t)=Ct^{-\nu}
for some
C\in\mathbb{C}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.24
.

Recall the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear maps
\mathfrak{j}
and
\mathfrak{z}
as defined in (
3.30
) and (
2.6
) respectively.
We have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{dd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times{\mathop{\rm E}}[\frac{X_{1}}{2}(X_{2}-\frac{1}{X_{2}})]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times t^{-\nu}}

(3.40)

in which
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}:=\mathcal{A}_{2}/\big[\mathcal{A}_{2}\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2}\partial_{2}+\nu)\big]
. Here the left vertical map
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
of (
3.40
) is given by (
3.17
) from Theorem
3.16
. Since the above diagram commutes up to a complex multiple, the sum
\sum_{n}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}
, being the image of
1
in
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
via the top-right path, is also a formal solution to the system of PDEs (
3.14
). Now the bottom-left path of (
3.40
) gives
t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]
which is a solution to (
3.14
) because of Lemma
3.25
and Lemma
3.26
. Since
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is holonomic, the
\mathbb{C}
-dimension of the local solution space of (
3.14
) equals the multiplicity of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
, which is one. So, in summary, we may regard (
3.36
) holds up to a complex scalar multiple
C_{\nu}
which can be identically zero. ∎

The next corollary determines the constant
C_{\nu}
that appears in (
3.36
)
for various classes of Bessel functions. Moreover, a connection between these generating functions and Sonine integrals, see e.g. Watson
[
64
, Chap. VI]
is established.

Corollary 3.27
.

(1)

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. Then

t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}.

(3.41)

(2)

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
. Then

e^{-i\pi/2}\,t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}Y_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}.

(3.42)

(3)

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
. Then

t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t+\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}I_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}.

(3.43)

(4)

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
. Then

i\pi\,t^{-\nu}\exp\big[-\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t+\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}K_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}.

(3.44)

where the notation “
\sim
” denotes the Borel resummation as defined in Definition
2.7
.

Remark 3.28
.

We note that the (
3.41
) and (
3.43
) above become well-known equalities when
\nu
is an integer.

Proof.

(1)

Suppose
\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}=J_{\nu+n}
in (
3.36
). We aim to show that
C_{\nu}=1
. In particular, we have

C_{\nu}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]-\frac{J_{0}(x)}{t}\sim\sum_{n=-\infty\atop n\not=0}^{+\infty}J_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n-1}.

(3.45)

That is, there is a
F\in\mathcal{O}_{dd}
20
20

20

It is clear that

\sum_{n=-\infty\atop n\not=0}^{+\infty}\frac{J_{\nu+n}(x)}{n}\,t^{n}

is a formal primitive of the right-side of (
3.45
).
such that

C_{\nu}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]-\frac{J_{0}(x)}{t}=\mathfrak{z}\big(\partial_{2}(J_{\nu+n}/n)\big)=\partial_{2}F

(3.46)

where the
z
-transform given by the Borel-resummation is defined in (
2.6
), and the
\partial_{2}
’s are defined in (
2.5
), where
\lim_{t\to-\infty}F(x,t)=0
if
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)>0
. According to Theorem
2.13
, the Borel transform
\mathfrak{B}
representing the
\mathfrak{z}
-transform is
\mathcal{A}
-linear, it follows that the left-side of (
3.45
) also has a primitive, which can be written as

\int\Big\{C_{\nu}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]-\frac{J_{\nu}(x)}{t}\Big\}\,dt.

Let
0<\delta<R
be given and
|\arg x|<\pi/2
. Consider the contour

\Gamma_{R,\delta}=(Re^{-i\pi},\,\delta e^{-i\pi})\cup\mathcal{C}_{\delta}\cup(\delta e^{i\pi},\,Re^{i\pi}),

where
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
denotes the circle centred at the origin with radius
\delta
. Thus the contour
\Gamma_{R,\delta}
can be considered a
truncated Hankel’s contour
which emanates from
-\infty
below the negative real-axis and then back to
-\infty
above the negative real-axis after circulating the origin once in an anti-clockwise direction.

Hence

C_{\nu}\int_{\Gamma_{R,\delta}}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt=\int_{\Gamma_{R,\delta}}\frac{J_{\nu}(x)}{t}\,dt

We now take the Cauchy principal values on both sides of this equation under the limits that
R\to+\infty
and
\delta\to 0
. The right-side yields

C_{\nu}\int_{\Gamma_{\infty,0}}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt=2\pi i\,J_{\nu}(x).

However, since the integral on the left-side converges to a Hankel contour, so we obtain

\int_{\Gamma_{R,\delta}}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt\longrightarrow\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt=2\pi i\,J_{\nu}(x)

as
R\to+\infty
and
\delta\to 0
, which actually equals to
2\pi iJ_{\nu}(x)
from the classical Schläfli-Sonine integrals which is valid for
|\arg x|<\frac{\pi}{2}
, see e.g., Watson
[
64
, Chap. VI, p. 176, (2)]
. This proves that
C_{\nu}=1
after comparing the above calculations. Alternatively, one can verify directly that the Hankel-type contour as the limit of
R\to 0,\,\delta\to 0
in (
3.46
) satisfies the Bessel equation (
1.19
) and the following asymptotic

\begin{split}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t=\frac{2x}{u}}}{{=}}\frac{x^{\nu}}{2^{\nu}}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}u^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[u-\dfrac{x^{2}}{4u}\big]\,du\\
&\approx\frac{x^{\nu}}{2^{\nu}}\frac{2^{\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)}=\frac{2^{\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)}\end{split}

when
x\to 0
. See also Watson
[
64
, pp. 175-176]
. Hence

\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt=2\pi i\,J_{\nu}(x).

Therefore, we deduce
C_{\nu}=1
. This proves (
3.41
).

(2)

Recall that

Y_{\nu+n}(x)=\frac{\cos(\nu+n)\pi\,J_{n+\nu}-J_{-\nu-n}}{\sin\nu\pi}=\cot\nu\pi\,J_{\nu+n}(x)-(-1)^{n}\csc\nu\pi\,J_{-\nu-n}(x).

Since
\big(J_{-\nu-n}(x)\big)_{n}
corresponds to PDEs (
3.14
) with
\nu
replaced by
-\nu
. Hence

t^{\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{-\nu-n}(x)\,t^{n}.

Thus

\begin{split}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}Y_{\nu+n}(x)&\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\big[\cot\nu\pi J_{\nu+n}(x)-(-1)^{n}\csc\nu\pi J_{-\nu-n}(x)\big]\,t^{n}\\
&=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\cot\nu\pi J_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}-\csc\nu\pi\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}J_{-\nu-n}(x)\,t^{n}\\
&=\cot\nu\pi\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}-\csc\nu\pi\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{-\nu+n}(x)\,(-1/t)^{n}\\
&=\cot\nu\pi t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]-\csc\nu\pi(-1/t)^{\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(-\dfrac{1}{t}+t)\big]\\
&=\big(\cot\nu\pi-(-1)^{\nu}\csc\nu\pi\big)t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\\
&=e^{-i\pi/2}t^{-\nu}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\end{split}

as asserted.

(3)

The proofs of (iii) and (iv) can be derived in a similar way as the proof of (ii) with the definitions
I_{\nu}(x)=J_{\nu}(ix)
and
K_{\nu}(x)=\frac{\pi}{2}(I_{-\nu}(x)-I_{\nu}(x))/\sin\nu\pi
.

∎

By an argument using the Cauchy integral formula, we can now ”extract” the
n
-th coefficient from the generating function (
3.41
) above to give a way to explain why the existence of the following integral representation of Bessel functions avoids using series expansions as in
[
66
, §17.231]
,
[
64
]
, see also
[
21
, §10.10-11]
.

Corollary 3.29

((
Schläfli-Sonine integrals
))
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
be given and
|\arg x|<\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}
. Then we have

J_{\nu+n}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1-n}\exp\big[\dfrac{x}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})\big]\,dt

(3.47)

holds for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

3.6.
Generating function of difference Bessel functions

Let us recall the Example
2.4
that
\mathcal{O}_{\vartriangle}
represents the space of analytic functions defined on a domain is a left
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module with

(\partial f)(x)=f(x+1)-f(x),\hskip 20.00003pt(Xf)(x)=xf(x-1),\hskip 10.00002ptf\in\mathcal{O}_{\vartriangle}.

We will occasionally use the well-known
Newton polynomials
written in falling factorial notations:

(x)_{0}=1,\hskip 10.00002pt(x)_{n}=\begin{cases}x(x-1)\cdots(x-n+1),&n\geq 1,\\
1/[(x+1)\cdots(x+n)]&n\leq-1.\end{cases}

For non-integer
\nu
, we adopt the notation

(x)_{\nu}=\frac{\Gamma(x+1)}{\Gamma(x+1-\nu)}.

(3.48)

Example 3.30

((
Difference Exponential function
))
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{C}
. We have seen in Example
3.3
that the Weyl exponential is any solution of the
D
-module
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)
. Now in particular, the
difference exponential function

\exp_{\Delta}(\cdot;a)
is defined as a solution of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)
in the
D
-module
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\exp_{\Delta}(\cdot;a)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}.

To obtain a binomial (or “Newton”) series expansion of
\exp_{\Delta}(\cdot;a)
, we factorize the above left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map as

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta},

in which
S=\sum{\frac{a_{k}}{k!}X^{k}}
as in (
3.1
). We can illustrate the above by the
commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\exp_{\Delta}(\cdot;a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 1}

(3.49)

As a result, we obtain

\exp_{\Delta}(x;a)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{k}}{k!}x(x-1)\cdots(x-k+1).

It is well-known that this binomial series converges absolutely to
(a+1)^{x}
for any complex
x
and any
|a|<1
, when an appropriate branch of logarithm is chosen.

Example 3.31

((
Difference trigonometric functions
))
.

We continue the list of producing difference versions of trigonometric functions from Example
3.4
, where the Weyl sine and the Weyl cosine are solutions of the
D
-module
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)
in a
D
-module
N
. Now in particular, we define the
difference sine function
and the
difference cosine function
to be solutions of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)
in the
D
-module
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
. To obtain binomial series expansions of these two functions, we use the factorization

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}

(3.50)

in which we respectively take
S=\sin X
and
S=\cos X
as in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
). This factorization can be illustrated by the
commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial^{2}+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\mathrm{Sin\,}X/\times\mathrm{Cos\,}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 1}

(3.51)

Thus we obtain

\sin_{\Delta}x:=\frac{1}{2i}[(1+i)^{x}-(1-i)^{x}]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(2k-1)!}(x)_{2k-1}

and

\cos_{\Delta}x:=\frac{1}{2}[(1+i)^{x}+(1-i)^{x}]=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2k)!}(x)_{2k}.
