These two series converge absolutely if and only if either
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)>0
or
x=0
, which are unlike the power series of the usual sine and cosine functions.

Example 3.32

((
Difference Bessel functions
))
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{1}
as before. Recall that the Weyl Bessel of order
\nu
(
3.6
) is any solution of the Bessel operator

L=(X\partial)^{2}+X^{2}-\nu^{2}

in some
D
-module
N
, in particular, we define the
difference Bessel function of order
\nu
, denoted by
J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
, to be a solution of
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L
in the
D
-module
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
. Recall the factorization from (
3.23
)

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(X\partial-\nu)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(x)_{\nu}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}_{\Delta},

(3.52)

in which

S=\mathcal{J}_{\nu}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}X^{2k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)}.

Thus we have obtained the Newton series expansion

J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x):=S((x)_{\nu})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(\nu+k+1)}(x)_{\nu+2k}.

(3.53)

J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
solves the second order difference equation (
1.18
) or in its equivalent form

[(x+2)^{2}-\nu^{2}]y(x+2)-(x+2)(2x+3)y(x+1)+2(x+2)(x+1)y(x)=0,

(3.54)

which is obtained from the action of the element
L=(X\partial)^{2}+(X^{2}-\nu^{2})
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, see Example
2.4
.

When
x\in\mathbb{Z}
, (
3.54
) and (
3.53
) are precisely the difference Bessel equation and the difference Bessel function
J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
that appear to be first studied by Bohner and Cuchta
[
4
]
21
21

21

Bohner and Cuchta
[
4
]
called
J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
the “Bessel difference function” and the equation (
3.54
) the “Bessel difference equation” instead.
. Either by replacing
\nu
in (
3.53
) by
-\nu
or by considering (
3.26
), we easily obtain

J^{\Delta}_{-\nu}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{-\nu+2k}k!\Gamma(-\nu+k+1)}(x)_{-\nu+2k},

(3.55)

so that
\{J^{\Delta}_{\nu},J^{\Delta}_{-\nu}\}
are
\mathbb{C}
-linearly independent solutions of (
3.54
) in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, provided that
2\nu\notin\mathbb{Z}
.

We now provide a statement about an half-plane convergence of the
J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
.

Proposition 3.33
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
be arbitrary. Then the difference Bessel function
J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
(
3.53
) converges uniformly in each compact subset of
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)>0
.

A justification of the above statement will be given in the Appendix §
A.1
. However, it was demonstrated in
[
4
, p. 1569]
that although the series
J^{\Delta}_{n}(x)
diverges for
x<-1
, it converges at
x=-1
. Thus, a natural question is about the convergence in the strip
\{x:-1<\mathop{\rm Re}x<-\frac{1}{2}\}
.

Similar to Example
3.34
, we have

Example 3.34
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and consider the bilateral sequence of classical Bessel functions
(J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n})_{n}
. Then the maps

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}^{+}:\mathcal{B}_{\nu}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}\end{array}

(3.56)

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}^{-}:\mathcal{B}_{\nu}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(J^{\Delta}_{-\nu-n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}\end{array}

(3.57)

are both left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear.
More generally, a general solution of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}
will be denoted by
\times\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
. That is,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}:\mathcal{B}_{\nu}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu+n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}.\end{array}

(3.58)

The following proposition and corollary are direct consequences of Corollary
3.8
with
N=\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}
.

Proposition 3.35

((
Delay-difference formulae)).

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}
be an arbitrary (bilateral sequence of) solution of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}\to\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}
as defined in (
3.58
). Then the
\mathrm{(}\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}\mathrm{)}

satisfies
the following delay-difference formulae

x\Delta\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x-1)+\nu\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)-x\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu-1}(x-1)=0

(3.59)

and

x\Delta\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x-1)-\nu\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)+x\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{\nu+1}(x-1)=0.

(3.60)

for each
n
.

The following three-term recurrence is a direct consequence of Corollary
3.10
with
N=\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
, or directly from the above two corollary.

Proposition 3.36

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

For every
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and every
x\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

2\nu\mathscr{C}_{\nu}^{\Delta}(x)-x\mathscr{C}_{\nu-1}^{\Delta}(x-1)-x\mathscr{C}_{\nu+1}^{\Delta}(x-1)=0.

(3.61)

One deduces the following formulae originally derived by Bohner and and Cuchta
[
4
, Theorems 5-6, Corollary 7]
about
J_{\nu}^{\Delta}
as consequences of the above when
\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}^{\Delta}(x)=J_{\nu+n}^{\Delta}(x)
for all
n
.

Corollary 3.37

((
[
4
, Theorems 5-6, Corollary 7]
))
.

For every
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and every
x\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle x\Delta J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x-1)+\nu J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)-xJ^{\Delta}_{\nu-1}(x-1)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.62)

\displaystyle x\Delta J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x-1)-\nu J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)+xJ^{\Delta}_{\nu+1}(x-1)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.63)

\displaystyle 2\nu J_{\nu}^{\Delta}(x)-xJ_{\nu-1}^{\Delta}(x-1)-xJ_{\nu+1}^{\Delta}(x-1)

\displaystyle=0.

(3.64)

Proof.

We provide an alternative proof based on the transmutation formulae from Proposition
3.6
. This more direct approach is similar to the derivation of the Corollary
3.7
. Thus it suffices to prove the (
3.62
). Since the right-side of (
3.7
) annihilates the
CJ^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
for some constant
C
, and the fact that on the Newton series (
3.53
) gives

\begin{split}(\partial+\nu/X)J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)&=J_{\nu}^{\Delta}(x)-J_{\nu}^{\Delta}(x-1)+\nu J_{\nu}^{\Delta}(x+1)/(x+1)\\
&=\frac{\Gamma(x+1)}{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(x+2-\nu)}\Big(\frac{2}{\nu}+O\big(\frac{1}{x}\big)\Big)\\
&=\frac{\Gamma(x+1)}{2^{\nu-1}\Gamma(\nu)\Gamma(x+2-\nu)}\Big(1+O\big(\frac{1}{x}\big)\Big)\\
\end{split}

as
x\approx 0
. On the other hand, according to the left-side of (
3.7
), the
(\partial+\nu/X)J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)
must be equal to
CJ^{\Delta}_{\nu-1}(x)
for some constant
C
. But

J_{\nu-1}^{\Delta}(x)\approx\frac{\Gamma(x+1)}{2^{\nu-1}\Gamma(\nu)\Gamma(x+2-\nu)}

as
x\approx 0
. One immediately deduces that
C=1
by comparing the above two asymptotes. This establishes the (
3.62
). The verification of (
3.63
) is similar. One can derive the (
3.64
) by subtracting the (
3.62
) and (
3.63
).
∎

Now we establish that a difference analogue of the generating function
e^{\frac{x}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})}
of the classical Bessel function for difference Bessel functions (
3.21
) is given by the function

\big[\frac{1}{2}\big(t-\frac{1}{t}\big)+1\big]^{x}

and to show that it serves as a generating function to integer order difference Bessel function to (
3.54
) in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
.

Theorem 3.38
.

Let
|\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})|<1
and
x\in\mathbb{C}
. Then

\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J^{\vartriangle}_{n}(x)\,t^{n}.

(3.65)

Proof.

Since
|\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})|<1
, so the series

\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{x}{k}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})\big]^{k}

is absolutely convergent.
Writing

\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})\Big]^{k}=\sum_{s=0}^{k}\binom{k}{s}(-\frac{1}{2t})^{s}(\frac{t}{2})^{k-s}.

Then

\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(x)_{k}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\frac{1}{(k-s)!s!}(-\frac{1}{2t})^{s}(\frac{t}{2})^{k-s}.

It is clear that the power of
t
goes from
-\infty
to
\infty
, as
k
goes to infinity. Then we exchange the order of summations to arrive at

\sum_{-\infty}^{\infty}t^{n}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{s}\frac{(x)_{n+2s}}{s!(n+s)!2^{n+2s}}=\sum_{-\infty}^{\infty}J^{\Delta}_{n}(x)t^{n}.

∎

The series manipulation technique used in the above proof is in the same spirit of that for the classical Bessel generating function (
3.21
) presented in
[
64
, §2.1]
. In order to deal with the generating function when
\nu\not=0
, we first need to study the asymptotic behaviour of
J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}
as
n\to\infty
. We apply the same technique of Banach algebra to
(J^{\Delta}_{n+\nu})
as in the proof of Theorem
3.22
with suitable modification.

Theorem 3.39

((
Asymptotic behaviour)).

The sequence
(J^{\Delta}_{n+\nu})
is a uniformly 1-Gevrey compacta solution of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

The proof makes use of the Newton transform
\mathfrak{N}
which is described in Theorem
5.1
. Fix a compact
K\subset\mathbb{C}
and for each
f\in\mathcal{O}_{d}
, one defines a norm by

|\|f|\|=\|\mathfrak{N}(f)\|_{L^{\infty},K},

where the right-side is the sup norm on the compact set
K
.
Then
(\mathcal{O}_{d},|\|\cdot|\|)
becomes a Banach algebra which can be verified easily.

The proof of Theorem
3.22
under this alternative norm shows that there exists
C>0
such that

|\|J_{\nu+n}|\|\leq Cn!(\dfrac{2}{|\|x|\|})^{n}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{for large }n.

Consequently, for large
n
,

\|J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}\|_{L^{\infty},K}=\|\mathfrak{N}(J_{\nu+n})\|_{L^{\infty},K}=|\|J_{\nu+n}|\|\leq Cn!(\dfrac{2}{|\|x|\|})^{n}.

∎

Remark 3.40
.

Similarly, the sequence
(J^{\Delta}_{\nu-n})
is also 1-Gevrey. The same is true if
J^{\Delta}
is replaced by
\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}
. As a consequence, the
z
-transform of the sequence
(J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n})
is a well-defined analytic function in two variables.

Theorem 3.41
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
.

(1)

Let
(\mathscr{C}^{\Delta}_{n+\nu})_{n}
be a bilateral sequence of analytic functions which is a solution of the Bessel module
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Then there exists a
1
-periodic function
C_{\nu}
such that

C_{\nu}(x)\,t^{-\nu}\big[\dfrac{1}{2}(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}^{\Delta}(x)\,t^{n},

(3.66)

where the symbol
\sim
means that the left-hand side is the Borel resummation
22
22

22

Definition
2.7
of the right-hand side whenever it diverges.

(2)

Moreover, the manifestation of the holonomic system of PDEs (
3.14
) in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
in Example
2.9
defined by (
2.4
) is given by

y(x+1,t)-y(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})y(x,t),\hskip 10.00002pt\nu y(x,t)+ty_{t}(x,t)=\frac{x}{2}\big(t+\frac{1}{t}\big)y(x-1,t).

(3.67)

To prove the above theorem, we need the following lemma, which serves the purpose of solving the holonomic system of PDEs that arise from
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
.

Lemma 3.42
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[\partial_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(1/{X_{2}}-X_{2})]+\mathcal{A}_{2}[2(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})-X_{1}(X_{2}+1/{X_{2}})]}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times t^{-\nu}[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1]^{x}}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}\end{array}

is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear map.

Proof.

It follows from the two generators of the
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
defined above that we solve the system of PDEs

\partial_{1}y+\frac{1}{2}(\dfrac{1}{X_{2}}-X_{2})y=0,\hskip 20.00003pt(\nu+X_{2}\partial_{2})y-\frac{X_{1}}{2}(\dfrac{1}{X_{2}}+X_{2})y=0

for an analytic function
y=y(x,\,t)
for the current choices of
\partial_{j},\,X_{j},\ j=1,\,2
. That is,

y(x+1,t)-y(x,t)=\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})y(x,t),\hskip 20.00003pt\nu y(x,t)+t\frac{\partial y}{dt}(x,t)=\frac{x}{2}(t+\frac{1}{t})y(x-1,t).

(3.68)

The first equation yields
y(x,t)=g(t)\big[\dfrac{1}{2}(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}
for some
g
. Substituting this
y
into the second differential-difference equation yields
g(t)=C_{\nu}t^{-\nu}
for some
C_{\nu}\not=0
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.41
.
Recall the maps
\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}
and
\mathfrak{z}_{\Delta}
as defined in (
3.58
) and (
2.7
) respectively. We have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\Delta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times{\mathop{\rm E}}\big[\frac{X_{1}}{2}\big(X_{2}-\frac{1}{X_{2}}\big)\big]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{z}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times t^{-\nu}}

(3.69)

in which
\widetilde{A}_{2}:=\overline{\mathcal{A}_{2}/\big[\mathcal{A}_{2}\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{2}\partial_{2}+\nu)\big]}
. Since the above diagram commutes up to a periodic multiple, the sum
\sum_{n}\mathscr{C}_{\nu+n}^{\Delta}(x)\,t^{n}
, being the image of
1
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
via the top right path, is also a formal solution to the system of PDEs (
3.14
). Now
t^{-\nu}\big[\dfrac{1}{2}(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}
is a solution to (
3.14
) because of Lemma
3.42
. Since
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
is holonomic, the
\mathbb{C}
-dimension of the local solution space of (
3.14
) equals the multiplicity of
\mathcal{B}_{\nu}
, which is one. So (
3.66
) holds up to a complex
1
-periodic function
C_{\nu}(x)
. ∎

Theorem 3.43
.

(1)

Let
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)<\mathop{\rm Re}(\nu)
. Then

e^{i\pi\nu}\frac{\sin(x-\nu)\pi}{\sin(\pi x)}\,t^{-\nu}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sim}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J^{\Delta}_{n+\nu}(x)\,t^{n},

(3.70)

where the notation “
\sim
” means that the left-side is the Borel-resummation of the right-side.

(2)

Moreover, the the generating function from part (i) satisfies the system of PDE (
3.68
)
23
23

23

See also the Appendix
C.5
.

Proof.

From Theorem
3.41
, we have

C_{\nu}(x)t^{-\nu}\big[\dfrac{1}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}\sim\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n}.

Subtracting
J_{\nu}(x)
from both sides and then dividing both sides by
t
, we obtain

C_{\nu}(x)t^{-\nu-1}\big[\dfrac{1}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}-\frac{J_{\nu}(x)}{t}\sim\sum_{n=-\infty,n\neq 0}^{\infty}J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}(x)\,t^{n-1}.

(3.71)

Now note that the right-side of (
3.71
) has an anti-
\partial_{2}
(e.g.,
\partial_{2}(\sum_{n=-\infty,n\neq 0}^{\infty}\frac{J^{\Delta}_{\nu+n}(x)}{n}\,t^{n})
). Since the Borel resummation is
\mathcal{A}
-linear, it follows that the LHS of (
3.71
) has an antiderivative.

Now let
0<\delta<R
be given and
|\arg x|<\pi/2
, and consider the contour

\Gamma_{R,\delta}=(Re^{-i\pi},\,\delta e^{-i\pi})\cup\mathcal{C}_{\delta}\cup(\delta e^{i\pi},\,Re^{i\pi}),

where
\mathcal{C}_{\delta}
denotes the circle centred at the origin with radius
\delta
. Thus the contour
\Gamma_{R,\delta}
can be considered a truncated Hankel’s contour which emanates from
-\infty
below the negative real-axis and then back to
-\infty
above the negative real-axis after circulating the origin once in an anti-clockwise direction. By Cauchy integral theorem, the integral of the left-side of (
3.71
) along
\Gamma_{R,\delta}
is zero, i.e.,

C_{\nu}(x)\int_{\Gamma_{R,\delta}}t^{-\nu-1}\big[\dfrac{1}{2}\big(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}\,dt=\int_{\Gamma_{R,\delta}}\frac{J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)}{t}\,dt=2\pi iJ^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x).

(3.72)

By (
3.73
) which is an analytic result to be obtained in the next subsection, we have

C_{\nu}(x)=e^{i\pi\nu}\frac{\sin(x-\nu)\pi}{\sin(\pi x)}.

∎

We remark that when
\nu=0
in the above theorem, one can recover the infinite sum in (
3.66
) from the generating function
\big[\dfrac{1}{2}(t-\dfrac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}
by Theorem
3.38
, thus showing that
C_{0}(x)\equiv 1
.

3.7.
Integral representations

Now we aim to find an integral representation of the difference Bessel functions that is analogous to the Sonine integral representation in Corollary
3.29
.

Theorem 3.44
.

Let
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
and
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)<\mathop{\rm Re}(\nu)
. Let
U
be a Hankel-type contour in the
t
-plane that starts from,
+\infty
above the real-axis, circles around the origin in the counter-clockwise direction, and returns to
+\infty
below the real-axis, and in particular

t^{-\nu}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}\bigg|_{\partial U}=0.

Then the integral

y_{\nu}(x)=\int_{U}t^{-\nu-1}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}\,dt

(1)

satisfies the delay-difference equations (
3.62
) and (
3.63
) above,

(2)

and, in particular, the integral solves the Bessel difference equation of order
\nu
(
3.54
):

x(x-1)\triangle^{2}y(x-2)+x\triangle y(x-1)+x(x-1)y(x-2)-\nu^{2}y(x)=0.

Proof.

We first note that because of
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)>\mathop{\rm Re}(-\nu)
, so that integral is a well-defined function of
x
.

(1)

We rewite the (
3.62
) and (
3.63
) in the equivalent forms

\begin{split}&2\triangle y_{\nu}(x)-y_{\nu-1}(x)+y_{\nu+1}(x)=0,\\
&2\nu y_{\nu}(x)-xy_{\nu-1}(x-1)-xy_{\nu+1}(x-1)=0.\end{split}

Then, it is easy to see that

2\triangle y_{\nu}(x)=\int_{U}(t^{-\nu}-t^{-\nu-2})\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}dt=y_{\nu-1}(x)-y_{\nu+1}(x)

holds. To verify the second equation, we substitute the
y_{n}
into the equation and apply integration-by-parts once to get

\begin{split}xy_{\nu-1}(x-1)+xy_{\nu+1}(x-1)&=x\int_{U}(t^{-\nu}+t^{-\nu-2})\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x-1}dt\\
&=2\int_{U}t^{-\nu}d\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}\\
&=-2\int_{U}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}dt^{-\nu}\\
&=2\nu y_{\nu}(x),\end{split}

as required

(2)

The proof to this part actually follows directly from the part (i) and Proposition
3.14
. However, we offer a direct verification.
Applying
\triangle
to the (
3.62
) and replacing
\nu
by
\nu-1
in the (
3.63
) respectively yield

\begin{split}&x\triangle^{2}y_{\nu}(x-1)+\triangle y_{\nu}(x)-x\triangle y_{\nu-1}(x-1)-y_{\nu-1}(x)+\nu\triangle y_{\nu}(x)=0,\\
&x\triangle y_{\nu-1}(x-1)+xy_{\nu}(x-1)-(\nu-1)y_{\nu-1}(x)=0.\end{split}

Adding the above two equations yields

x\triangle^{2}y_{\nu}(x-1)+\triangle y_{\nu}(x)+xy_{\nu}(x-1)-\nu y_{\nu-1}(x)+\nu\triangle y_{\nu}(x)=0.

Replacing
x
by
x-1
, and multiplying
x
throughout both sides of the above equation yield

x(x-1)\triangle^{2}y_{\nu}(x-2)+x\triangle y_{\nu}(x-1)+x(x-1)y_{\nu}(x-2)-x\nu y_{\nu-1}(x-1)+x\nu\triangle y_{\nu}(x-1)=0.

Multiplying by
\nu
on both sides of (
3.62
) and substitute the resulting equation into the last expression yield precisely the Bessel difference equation of order
\nu
(
3.54
).

∎

Theorem 3.45

((
Difference Schläfli-Sonine integrals
))
.

Let
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)<\mathop{\rm Re}(\nu)
. Let
U
be an Hankel-type contour parametrised by
t
that starts at
+\infty
, above the real-axis, circles around the origin in the counter-clockwise direction, and returns to
+\infty
below the real-axis, and such that
|2t+t^{2}|\geq 1
on
U
. Then

J^{\Delta}_{\nu}(x)=e^{i\pi\nu}\dfrac{\sin(x-\nu)\pi}{\sin(\pi x)}\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}dt

(3.73)

Proof.

The integrand has branch points
0,\,-1\pm\sqrt{2}
and
\infty
when
\nu,x
are not integers. We pick the branch cuts from
[-1-\sqrt{2},\,0]
and
[-1+\sqrt{2},\,+\infty]
on the real-axis. Since
|2t+t^{2}|\geq 1
on
U
, the integrand allows an absolutely convergent expansion

\begin{split}t^{-\nu-1}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}&=t^{-\nu-1}(1+\frac{t}{2})^{x}\big[1-\frac{1}{2t(1+t/2)}\big]^{x}\\
&=t^{-\nu-1}(1+\frac{t}{2})^{x}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{x}{k}(-1)^{k}[\frac{1}{2t(1+t/2)}]^{k}.\\
\end{split}

Observe that the
lowest
term of (
3.53
) has

\frac{2^{-\nu}\Gamma(x+1)}{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(x+1-\nu)}\approx\frac{1}{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(1-\nu)},

as
x\approx 0
.

Next we consider the lowest term of

\begin{split}&\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}\big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\big]^{x}dt\\
&=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}(1+\frac{t}{2})^{x}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{x}{k}(-1)^{k}[\frac{1}{2t(1+t/2)}]^{k}.\\
\end{split}

That is,

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}(1+\frac{t}{2})^{x}dt.

Let
\frac{t}{2}=e^{i\pi}u
in above integration. Then we have

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}e^{-\nu\pi i}2^{-\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}u^{-\nu-1}(1-u)^{x}\,du.

We recall a classical result about beta function, see for example
[
63
, p. 104 (12)]
, in an Hankel-type contour which happens to match exactly with the change of variable
t=2e^{i\pi}u
above, namely that

\dfrac{\Gamma(p+q+1)}{\Gamma(p+1)\Gamma(q+1)}=\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-p-1}(1-t)^{-q-1}dt,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\rm Re}(p+q+1)>0

holds. Hence we obtain

\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}e^{-\nu\pi i}2^{-\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}u^{-\nu-1}(1-u)^{x}du=e^{-\nu\pi i}2^{-\nu}\dfrac{\Gamma(\nu-x)}{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(-x)}

which is
valid for
\mathop{\rm Re}(x-\nu)<0
. Since

\dfrac{\Gamma(\nu-x)}{\Gamma(-x)}=\dfrac{\sin(-x)\pi\,\Gamma(x+1)}{\sin(\nu-x)\pi\,\Gamma(x+1-\nu)}.

Hence we obtain

e^{i\pi\nu}\dfrac{\sin(x-\nu)\pi}{\sin(\pi x)}\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}t^{-\nu-1}(1+\frac{t}{2})^{x}dt=\frac{2^{-\nu}\Gamma(x+1)}{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(x+1-\nu)}\approx\frac{2^{-\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu+1)\Gamma(1-\nu)},

as
x\approx 0
which matches the coefficient of the lowest term of the other side. This implies that
both sides of (
3.73
) are the same and (
3.73
) holds. ∎

We can modify the Hankel-type contour
U
in the integral considered above to obtain a
second linearly independent solution to the difference Bessel equation (
3.54
).

Theorem 3.46
.

Let
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)>\mathop{\rm Re}(\nu+1)
. Let
U^{\prime}
be an Hankel-type contour parametrised by
s=-1/t
where
t
parametrised by the contour
U
as is defined in Theorem
3.45
. Then

J^{\Delta}_{-\nu}(x)=e^{i\pi}e^{-2i\pi\nu}\dfrac{\sin(x+\nu)\pi}{\sin\pi x}\dfrac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{U^{\prime}}s^{-\nu-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(s-\frac{1}{s})+1\Big]^{x}ds.

(3.74)

Proof.

Notice that both ends of the contour
U^{\prime}
would be at the origin which corresponds to
U
at
+\infty
. The restriction
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)>\mathop{\rm Re}(\nu+1)
ensures that the integral converges at the origin. Substituting
t=-1/s
into the contour integral yields

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{U}(-t)^{\nu-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}dt=\frac{-e^{i\nu\pi}}{2\pi i}\int_{U}t^{\nu-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}(t-\frac{1}{t})+1\Big]^{x}dt

where we need to be more restrictive under the extra temporary assumption that
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)<\mathop{\rm Re}(-\nu)
holds in order to ensure that the integral converges at
+\infty
.
Then the proof of the (
3.74
) follows closely that of the last theorem. One then considers the
lowest term
from the integral and to compare it with that of the
J^{\Delta}_{-\nu}(x)
in (
3.55
).
We can now remove the extra assumption
\mathop{\rm Re}(x)<\mathop{\rm Re}(-\nu)
.
∎

4.
Half-Bessel modules I: Bessel polynomials

We recall the formula

K_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\,e^{-x}x^{-n-\frac{1}{2}}\theta_{n}(x)

(4.1)

that connects the modified Bessel function (or Macdonald function) of order
n+\frac{1}{2}
and the
reversed Bessel polynomials

\theta_{n}(x)
.
The modified Bessel functions
I_{\nu}(x)
and
K_{\nu}(x)
are essentially related to
J_{\nu}(ix)
and
Y_{\nu}(ix)
respectively. We refer to Watson
[
64
]
for their precise definitions so that both
I_{\nu}(x)
and
K_{\nu}(x)
are real on the real axis. We first modify the adopt the transmutation formulae for the Bessel modules from subsection §
3.2
to half-Bessel modules that better suit our purpose of discussion in this section. Thus, it is more natural to discuss the reverse Bessel polynomials
\theta_{n}(x)
than the Bessel polynomials
y_{n}(x)=x^{n}\theta_{n}(1/x)
from our
{D}
-modules viewpoint. So the discussion of the
y_{n}(x)
and its difference analogue will be postponed to the end of this section.

4.1.
Transmutation formulae

Replacing
\partial
by
-i\partial
and
X
by
iX
, we still have
[-i\partial,\,iX]=1
. So the transmutation formulae in Proposition
3.6

becomes

Lemma 4.1
.

For each
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
,

\displaystyle[(X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+(\nu+1)^{2})]\left(\partial-\dfrac{\nu}{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial-\dfrac{\nu+2}{X}\right)[(X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+\nu^{2})],

(4.2)

\displaystyle[(X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+(\nu-1)^{2})]\left(\partial+\dfrac{\nu}{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial+\dfrac{\nu-2}{X}\right)[(X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+\nu^{2})],

(4.3)

which induce the left
\mathcal{A}
-linear maps

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+(\nu+1)^{2}))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\partial-\frac{\nu}{X})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+\nu^{2})),\\
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+(\nu-1)^{2}))&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\partial+\frac{\nu}{X})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}((X\partial)^{2}-(X^{2}+\nu^{2})).\end{array}

As a result we deduce from this corollary the following well-known formulae with the help of the asymptotic of
K_{\nu}(x)
as
x\to 0
.

Corollary 4.2
.

For each
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
, the modified Bessel functions
K_{\nu}(x)
satisfies the following recurrence relations.

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{\nu}(x)-\dfrac{\nu K_{\nu}(x)}{x}

\displaystyle=-K_{\nu+1}(x),

\displaystyle K^{\prime}_{\nu}(x)+\dfrac{\nu K_{\nu}(x)}{x}

\displaystyle=-K_{\nu-1}(x).

The formula (
4.1
) suggests the following “change of variable” of
X\partial
to
X\partial-X-\nu
and keep the
X
unchanged that the transmutation formulae in Lemma
4.1
become

Proposition 4.3

((
Transmutation formulae
))
.

For each
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu+\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial

\displaystyle+2(\nu+\frac{1}{2})\Big)(X\partial-X-2\nu)

\displaystyle=\big(X\partial-X-2(\nu+\frac{1}{2})\big)\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu-\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial+2(\nu-\frac{1}{2})\Big),

(4.4)

\displaystyle\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu-\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial

\displaystyle+2(\nu-\frac{1}{2})\Big)\frac{1}{X}(\partial-1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{X}(\partial-1-\frac{1}{X})\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu+\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial+2(\nu+\frac{1}{2})\Big),

(4.5)

which induce the left
\mathcal{A}
-linear maps

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu+\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial+2(\nu+\frac{1}{2})\Big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(X\partial-X-2\nu)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu-\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial+2(\nu-\frac{1}{2})\Big),\\
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu-\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial+2(\nu-\frac{1}{2})\Big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\frac{1}{X}(\partial-1)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\Big(X\partial^{2}-2(\nu+\frac{1}{2}+X)\partial+2(\nu+\frac{1}{2})\Big).\end{array}

4.2.
Reverse Bessel polynomial modules

4.2.1.
Reverse Bessel polynomial operator

Some of Burchnall’s formulae found in
[
8
]
are applications of the Weyl-algebraic formulae to be derived in this section. They are special cases in the manifestation of
D
-modules
\mathcal{O}_{d}
. We shall change the manifestation of
D
-modules to
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
when we treat the difference Bessel polynomials later. We are ready to set
\nu=n+\frac{1}{2}
and
\nu=n-\frac{1}{2}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
in (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) respectively to obtain

Corollary 4.4
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following are well-defined left
\mathcal{A}
-linear maps.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n+1+X)\partial+2(n+1)\big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(X\partial-X-2n-1)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n\big),\\
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n-1+X)\partial+2(n-1)\big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\frac{1}{X}(\partial-1)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n\big).\end{array}

Let
\Theta_{0}=1
and consider for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the gauge transformations defined by right multiplication by

G_{n}:=-(X\partial-X-2n-1),\hskip 10.00002ptn\in\mathbb{Z}.\mod X\partial

Then we define

\Theta_{n}(X)=G_{n-1}\,\cdots G_{1}\,G_{0}\,\Theta_{0}(X)\mod X\partial

(4.6)

to be the
reversed Bessel polynomial of degree

n
.

Theorem 4.5
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the map

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\Theta_{n}(X)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2X\partial\big)

where
\Theta_{n}(X)
is defined in (
4.6
) is a well-defined left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map.

In particular, we have

\begin{split}\Theta_{1}(X)&=G_{0}\,\Theta_{0}(X)=(-X\partial+X+1)=X+1\quad\mod X\partial\\
\Theta_{2}(X)&=G_{1}\,G_{0}\,\Theta_{0}(x)=(-X\partial+X+3)\,(X+1)=X^{2}+3X+3,\quad\mod X\partial\\
\Theta_{3}(X)&=G_{2}\,G_{1}\,G_{0}\,\Theta_{0}(x)(-X\partial+x-5)X^{2}+3X+3=X^{3}+6X^{2}+15X+15,\quad\mod X\partial\\
\cdots&\cdots\end{split}

(4.7)

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\Theta_{n}(X)=G_{n-1}\cdots G_{1}G_{0}\Theta_{0}(X)
. Then we have

\Theta_{n}(X)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{2^{k}(n-k)!\,k!}X^{n-k},

(4.8)

and the map

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n)\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\Theta_{n}(X)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial

(4.9)

is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear.

On the other hand, let
\Theta_{0}=1
and consider the gauge transformation
H
defined by a left multiplication by the element
-\frac{1}{X}(\partial-1)
. Then we have

1=\Theta_{0}(X)=H^{n}(\Theta_{n}(X))=(H\,\cdots\,H)(\Theta_{n}(X)).

(4.10)

That is,

\begin{split}\Theta_{0}(X)&=H(\Theta_{1}(X))=1/X(1-\partial)(X+1)\quad\mod\mathcal{A}\partial\\
\Theta_{0}(X)&=H^{2}(\Theta_{2}(X))=\big[1/X(1-\partial)\big]^{2}(X^{2}+3X+3),\quad\mod\mathcal{A}\partial\\
\Theta_{0}(X)&=H^{3}(\Theta_{3}(X))=\big[1/X(1-\partial)\big]^{3}(X^{3}+6X^{2}+15X+15),\quad\mod\mathcal{A}\partial\\
\cdots&\cdots\end{split}

(4.11)

Corollary 4.7
.

For each
n
, let
\Theta_{n}
be defined as above, which is a solution of
X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n
in the left
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathbb{C}[X]
. Then

\displaystyle(X\partial-X-2n-1)\Theta_{n}+\Theta_{n+1}=0,

\displaystyle(\partial-1)\Theta_{n}+X\Theta_{n-1}=0.

as elements in
M
.

Proof.

We only proof the first expression. The second expression can be dealt with similarly. Let
\nu=n+\frac{1}{2}
in (
4.4
). Then we note that the right-side of (
4.4
) annihilates the
\Theta_{N}(X)
, while the left-side of (
4.4
) therefore implies that

(X\partial-X-(2n+1))\Theta_{n}(X)=C\Theta_{n+1}(X),\mod\mathcal{A}\partial

for some constant
C
. A simple comparison of the highest terms on both sides yields that
C=-1
. This proves the desired result.
∎

We immediately deduce from the above Corollary the following recursion.

Corollary 4.8

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

For each
n
, let
\Theta_{n}
be defined as above, which is a solution of
X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n
in the left
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathbb{C}[X]
. Then

\Theta_{n+1}-(2n+1)\Theta_{n}-X^{2}\Theta_{n-1}=0

as an element in
M
.

We next show that the reverse Bessel polynomial equation
[
32
, p. 8]
or more generally the
half-order Bessel polynomial operator

L_{n}=X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n

can be studied as a special case of the
half-Bessel module
below. The construction of this module is motivated by Corollary
4.7
and the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure endowed on
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}

as in Example
2.15
.

Definition 4.9
.

We define the
reverse Bessel polynomial module
to be the
modified half-Bessel module to be the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module

\Theta=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\partial_{1}-1+{X_{1}}/{\partial_{2}})+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}\partial_{1}-2X_{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}+\partial_{2})}.

(4.12)

We first verify that the Bessel polynomial module
\Theta
defined above is holomonic.

Theorem 4.10
.

The left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module
\Theta
has dimension two and multiplicity two. In particular,
\Theta
is holonomic.

Proof.

Multiply
\partial_{2}
throughout the first generator from (
4.12
) yields

\partial_{2}\partial_{1}-\partial_{2}+X_{1}

before eliminating
\partial_{1}
from the second generator from the (
4.12
)

X_{1}\partial_{1}-2X_{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}+\partial_{2}.

This yields

\begin{split}\partial_{2}\partial_{1}-\partial_{2}+{X_{1}}&=\partial_{2}\big(\frac{2X_{2}}{X_{1}}\partial_{2}-\frac{1}{X_{2}}\partial_{2}+1+1/X_{1}\big)-\partial_{2}+X_{1}\\
&=((2X_{1}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2})/X_{1}\end{split}

(4.13)

Thus we can replace the original two generators by

((2X_{1}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2})/X_{1},\hskip 10.00002pt\partial_{1}-(2X_{2}+1)/X_{1}\partial_{2}-1-1/X_{1}.

This renders the elements in the
\Gamma^{k}\Theta
are spanned by
\{X_{1}^{a}X_{2}^{b}:a+b\leq k\}
and

\{X_{1}^{a^{\prime}{}}X_{2}^{b^{\prime}{}}\partial_{2}:a+b\leq k-1\}
. Hence the
\Theta
shares the same Hilbert polynomial with that studied in Example
2.26
so that

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{Gr}^{k}M=k^{2}.

It follows that the
\Theta
is holonomic, hence dimension two and with multiplicity two.
∎

Eliminating
\partial_{1}
again from the above two relations of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
from the Definition
\Theta
(
4.9
) yields the following element of
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module.

Proposition 4.11

((
Three-term recursion
))
.

The natural map

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[(2X_{1}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}]}\longrightarrow\Theta

is a well-defined left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear surjection.

The following records the relationship between the half-Bessel module
\Theta
defined above and the second order half-Bessel operator
L_{n}
.

Proposition 4.12

((
Weyl-algebraic reverse Bessel-polynomial sequence of
\Theta
))
.

The natural map

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[X_{1}\partial_{1}^{2}-2(X_{1}+X_{2}\partial_{2})\partial_{1}+2X_{2}\partial_{2}]}\longrightarrow\Theta

is a well-defined left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear surjection.

The derivation of this result is given in the Appendix
A.2
.

Proof.

See the Appendix.
∎

4.2.2.
Classical reverse Bessel polynomials

To study Poisson generating functions, i.e., generating functions of the form
\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbb{N}}a_{n}{t^{n}}/{n!}
, one uses the
D
-module structure endowed on spaces
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of (one-sided) sequences as in Example
2.15
. The purpose of the section is to illustrate that many classically known defining formulae about the reverse Bessel polynomials can be derived and presented in a coherent manner as consequences of manifestation of the
D
-module structures discussed in the last section. We shall show that the same
D
-module structure with a different manifestation would allow us to reach an analogous theory for difference reverse Bessel polynomials in the next subsection. Thus except for the transmutation formulae derived from above, the remaining formulae that we derive here are well-known in the classical Bessel polynomial literature, e.g.,
[
32
]
. Reader who are looking for new formulae about difference Bessel polynomials may proceed directly to the next section.

We deduce from Theorem
4.6
the expected result:

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\begin{array}[]{rclll}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n)\big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\Theta_{n}(X)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}\end{array}

sends
1
to the classical reversed Bessel polynomial

\theta_{n}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{2^{k}(n-k)!\,k!}\,x^{n-k}.

Example 4.14
.

Consider the sequence of classical Bessel polynomials
(\theta_{n})
. Then the maps

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}:\Theta&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\theta_{n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\end{array}

(4.14)

is well-defined and left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear.

The following differential-difference equations and three-term recurrence, as well as Corollaries
4.7
and
4.8
about classical Bessel polynomials below present solution of the module
\Theta
in
\mathcal{O}_{d}
. They follow from Example
4.14
, hence giving new proofs to these classical results. We shall see how solutions of the module
\Theta
in
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
gives their difference analogues below.

Theorem 4.15

((
differential-difference equations

[
32
]
, p. 19))
.

The reverse classical Bessel polynomials
\{\theta_{n}(x)\}
satisfy the following (well-known) formulae

\begin{split}&\theta_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\theta_{n}(x)+x\theta_{n-1}(x)=0,\\
&x\theta_{n}^{\prime}(x)-(x+2n+1)\theta_{n}(x)+\theta_{n+1}(x)=0\end{split}

(4.15)

for all
n\geq 1
and
x
.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
4.7
with the manifestation of analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{d}
as a
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module given by the Example
2.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.16

((
Three-term recurrence

[
32
]
, p. 18))
.

The sequence
(\theta_{n})
satisfies the (well-known) three-term recurrence formula

\theta_{n+2}(x)-(2n+3)\theta_{n+1}(x)-x^{2}\theta_{n}(x)=0

(4.16)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and every
x
.

Proof.

This follows from the last Corollary or directly from the Corollary
4.8
with the manifestation of the
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module
\mathcal{O}_{d}
as given by the Example
2.3
.
∎

Recently, there are also complex analytic studies of the PDEs satisfied by generating functions of some classical special functions, including the Bessel polynomials
[
34
,
33
]
.

4.2.3.
Difference reverse Bessel polynomials

Now we consider the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure of
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
as in Example
2.16
. Recalling that
\Theta_{n}
is a solution of
L_{n}:=X\partial^{2}-2(n+X)\partial+2n
, we immediately obtain the following

Theorem 4.17

((
Difference equation
))
.

For each integer
n\geq 0
, let
\theta^{\Delta}_{n}
be the difference reverse Bessel polynomial of degree
n
defined by

\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{2^{k}(n-k)!\,k!}\,(x)_{n-k}.

(4.17)

Then for each integer
n\geq 0
and each
x
, we have

(x-2n)\,\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x+1)-4(x-n)\,\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x)+3x\,\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x-1)=0.

(4.18)

The following delay-differential relations and three-term recurrence are direct consequences of the module
\Theta
, as well as Corollaries
4.7
and
4.8
with
N=\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
.

Theorem 4.18

((
Delay-difference relations
))
.

The sequence of difference reverse Bessel polynomials
(\theta^{\Delta}_{n})
satisfy the following two delay-difference equations

\begin{split}\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x+1)-2\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)+x\theta^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-1)=0,\\
\theta^{\Delta}_{n+1}(x)+(x-2n-1)\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)-2x\,\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x-1)=0,\end{split}

(4.19)

for every
n\geq 1
and every
x
.

Proof.

This follows from Corollary
4.7
with the manifestation of the analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
as a
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module given by the Example
2.4
.
∎

It follows from Proposition
4.11
and the structure of the above
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module the new three-term recursion formula for the reverse difference Bessel polynomials.

Theorem 4.19

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

The sequence of difference reversed Bessel polynomials
(\theta^{\Delta}_{n})
satisfies the following recurrence relation

\theta^{\Delta}_{n+2}(x)-(2n+3)\theta^{\Delta}_{n+1}(x)-x(x-1)\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x-2)=0

(4.20)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and every
x
.

Proof.

This follows from the last Corollary or directly from the Corollary
4.8
with the manifestation of the analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{d}
as a
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module given by the Example
2.4
.
∎

4.3.
Characteristics of modified half-Bessel modules

We recall from (
4.12
) the definition of the half-Bessel module. We may change one of the two generators from the half-Bessel module (
4.12
) by
(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}
, as suggested by Corollary
4.8
.

Theorem 4.20
.

The half-Bessel module
\Theta
is isomorphic to the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}]+\mathcal{A}_{2}[X_{1}\partial_{1}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}]}.

Theorem 4.21
.

The map

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}[(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}]+\mathcal{A}_{2}[X_{1}\partial_{1}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}]}\longrightarrow\Theta

is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear isomorphism.

Theorem 4.22
.

Let

\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho):=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho,{1}/{\rho})}{\langle\rho^{2}-1+2X_{2}\rangle}

and let
S
be the element

S=C_{1}\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1-\rho)]+C_{2}\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1+\rho)],

(4.21)

for every choice of
C_{1},\,C_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, where the
{\mathop{\rm E}}
is the Weyl exponential defined in Example
3.3
. Then the map

\begin{split}\Theta(\rho):=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)[(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}]+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)[X_{1}\partial_{1}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}]}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)\partial_{2}}}\end{split}

(4.22)

is left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-linear.

Proof.

Let

\Lambda_{1}=\partial_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\Lambda_{2}=\rho\partial_{2},\hskip 10.00002ptZ_{1}=X_{1},\hskip 10.00002ptZ_{2}=-\rho,

(4.23)

where
\rho^{2}=1-2X_{2}
. Since

\partial_{2}\rho-\rho\partial_{2}=-\frac{1}{\rho},

we deduce from direct computations that

[\Lambda_{1},Z_{1}]=1,\hskip 10.00002pt[\Lambda_{2},Z_{2}]=1,\hskip 10.00002pt[\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}]=0,\hskip 10.00002pt[Z_{1},Z_{2}]=0,\hskip 10.00002pt[\Lambda_{i},\,Z_{j}]=0,\hskip 10.00002pti\not=j

(4.24)

hold.

It is routine to check that

(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}=-\Lambda_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{Z_{2}}\Lambda_{2}+Z_{1}^{2}.

(4.25)

Let us make use of the characteristic change of elements by (
4.23
) in the second generator
X_{1}\partial_{1}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}
to obtain

Z_{1}\Lambda_{1}-Z_{2}\Lambda_{2}-1-Z_{1}=Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)-\Lambda_{2}Z_{2}.

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}=\langle\Lambda_{1},\,\Lambda_{2},\,Z_{1},\,Z_{2}\rangle
and

\widehat{\Theta}:=\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}{\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}[(Z_{2}\Lambda_{2}^{2}+2\Lambda_{2}-Z_{2}Z_{1}^{2}]+\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}[(Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)-\Lambda_{2}Z_{2})]}

Note that the above quotient
\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}
-module
\widehat{\Theta}
is holonomic with dimension and multiplicity both equal to
2
, see e.g., Example
2.26
.

In order to find the map

\widehat{\Theta}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times\widehat{S}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}{{\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}\Lambda_{1}+{\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{2}}}\Lambda_{2}}},

we
left multiply the expression (
4.25
) by
Z_{2}
. Further simplification yield

\begin{split}(Z_{2}\Lambda_{2})\Lambda_{2}+2\Lambda_{2}-Z_{2}Z_{1}^{2}&=\Lambda_{2}^{2}Z_{2}-Z_{2}Z_{1}^{2}\\
&=(\Lambda_{2}^{2}-Z_{1}^{2})Z_{2}\\
&=(\Lambda_{2}+Z_{1})(\Lambda_{2}-Z_{1})Z_{2}.\end{split}

Since the two first-order operators above commute, so we deduce that

\frac{1}{Z_{2}}F(Z_{1})E(Z_{1}Z_{2})+\frac{1}{Z_{2}}G(Z_{1})E(-Z_{1}Z_{2})

is the general solution to the above operator
for some
F(Z_{1})
and
G(Z_{1})
. This is a sum of two solutions which also conform with our knowledge that the
\widehat{\Theta}
has multiplicity
2
discussed above.

We now make use of the second generator of
\widehat{\Theta}
to determine the exact forms of the solutions
F
and
G
. We first note that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})=Z_{2}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})+{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{1},

(4.26)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})=Z_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})+{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{2}.

(4.27)

It follows from (
4.26
) and (
4.27
) that

\begin{split}&\big(Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)-\Lambda_{2}Z_{2}\big)\frac{1}{Z_{2}}F(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&=Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)F(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})-\Lambda_{2}F(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&=(Z_{1}/Z_{2})(\Lambda_{1}-1)F(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})-F(Z_{1})Z_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})-F(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{2}\\
&=(Z_{1}/Z_{2})(\Lambda_{1}-1)F(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})-F(Z_{1})Z_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\quad\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{2}\\
&=(Z_{1}/Z_{2})\big({\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{1}F(Z_{1})-F(Z_{1})\Lambda_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\big)-(Z_{1}/Z_{2})F(Z_{1})E(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&\qquad-F(Z_{1})Z_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&=(Z_{1}/Z_{2}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{1}F(Z_{1})-(Z_{1}/Z_{2})F(Z_{1})Z_{2}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})-(Z_{1}/Z_{2})F(Z_{1})E(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&\qquad-F(Z_{1})Z_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&=(Z_{1}/Z_{2}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{1}F(Z_{1})-(Z_{1}/Z_{2})F(Z_{1})E(Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&=(Z_{1}/Z_{2}){\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1}Z_{2})(\Lambda_{1}-1)F(Z_{1})\\
&=0\quad\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{1}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{2},\end{split}

where the last equal sign holds provided that

(\Lambda_{1}-1)F(Z_{1})=0\hskip 10.00002pt\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{1}.

But this equation could hold when we have chosen
F(Z_{1})=-{\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1})
, see Example
3.3
, except perhaps for a constant multiple.

It remains to consider the following and a similar calculation yields

\begin{split}&\big(Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)-\Lambda_{2}Z_{2}\big)\frac{1}{Z_{2}}G(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&={Z_{1}}/{Z_{2}}\Big({\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{1}G(Z_{1})-Z_{2}{\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})G(Z_{1})\Big)-{Z_{1}}/{Z_{2}}G(Z_{1}){\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})\\
&\qquad-\Big({\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})\Lambda_{2}G(Z_{1})-Z_{1}{\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})G(Z_{1})\Big)\qquad\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{1}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{2}\\
&={\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})/Z_{2}\Big(Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)+Z_{2}\Lambda_{2}\Big)G(Z_{1})\qquad\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{1}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{2}\\
&=(1/Z_{2}){\mathop{\rm E}}(-Z_{1}Z_{2})Z_{1}(\Lambda_{1}-1)G(Z_{1})\\
&=0\qquad\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{1}+\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{2},\end{split}

where the last equal sign would hold if

G(Z_{1})={\mathop{\rm E}}(Z_{1})\hskip 10.00002pt\mod\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\Lambda_{1},

which may differ by a constant multiple. This establishes the (
4.21
) and hence completes the proof.
∎

4.4.
Bessel polynomials module

In this subsection, we define the
Weyl Bessel polynomial of degree

n\geq 0
by

Y_{n}(X)=X^{n}\Theta_{n}(1/X)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{(n-k)!k!2^{k}}X^{k}.

(4.28)

Right multiplication by
Y_{n}(X)
gives a left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(X^{2}\partial^{2}+(2X+2)\partial-n(n+1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times Y_{n}(X)\ \ }}{{\longrightarrow}}\overline{\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial}.

We may further define when
n=-1
, then
Y_{-1}(X)=1
.

We deduce from Corollary
4.7
the followings.

Corollary 4.23
.

For each
n
, let
Y_{n}
be defined as above, which is a solution of
X^{2}\partial^{2}+(2X+2)\partial-n(n+1)
in a certain left
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathbb{C}[X]
. Then

\displaystyle(X^{2}\partial+(n+1)X+1)Y_{n}-Y_{n+1}=0,

(4.29)

\displaystyle(X^{2}\partial-nX+1)Y_{n}-Y_{n-1}=0.

(4.30)

as elements in
N
.

Proof.

Replacing
X
by
1/X
and
\partial
by
-X^{2}\partial
in Corollary
4.7
, we obtain

\displaystyle(-X\partial-1/X-2n-1)\Theta_{n}(1/X)+\Theta_{n+1}(1/X)=0,

\displaystyle(-X^{2}\partial-1)\Theta_{n}(1/X)+(1/X)\Theta_{n-1}(1/X)=0.

Left-multiplying both sides of the first equation by
X^{n+1}
and both sides of the second equation by
X^{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(-X^{2}\partial-1-(n+1)X)X^{n}\Theta_{n}(1/X)+X^{n+1}\Theta_{n+1}(1/X)=0,

\displaystyle(-X^{2}\partial-1+nX)X^{n}\Theta_{n}(1/X)+X^{n-1}\Theta_{n-1}(1/X)=0,

and hence the result follows.
∎

Corollary 4.24

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

For each
n
, let
Y_{n}
be defined as above, which is a solution of
X^{2}\partial^{2}+(2X+2)\partial-n(n+1)
in a certain left
\mathcal{A}
-module
C[x]
. Then

Y_{n+1}-(2n+1)XY_{n}-Y_{n-1}=0

(4.31)

as an element in
N
.

We introduce
(\partial_{2}f)_{n}=f_{n+1}
and
(X_{2}f)_{n}=nf_{n-1}
to constrruct an
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module, such that

[\partial_{1},X_{1}]=1,\hskip 10.00002pt[\partial_{2},X_{2}]=1,\hskip 10.00002pt[X_{1},X_{2}]=0,\hskip 10.00002pt[\partial_{1},\partial_{2}]=0.

Replacing
n
by
n-1
in (
4.29
) and representing the resulting formula in terms of
X_{1},\partial_{i},i=1,2
yield

X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}+X_{1}X_{2}\partial_{2}+1-\partial_{2}.

(4.32)

Similarly, the equation (
4.30
) can be rewritten as

X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}-X_{1}X_{2}\partial_{2}+1-\partial_{2}^{-1}.

Right multiplying
\partial_{2}
to the above formula gives

X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}-X_{1}X_{2}\partial^{2}_{2}+\partial_{2}-1.

(4.33)

Thus the
Bessel polynomial module
is given by

Definition 4.25
.

The
Bessel polynomial module
is the left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module defined by

\mathcal{Y}=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}\partial_{2}-X_{1}X_{2}\partial^{2}_{2}+\partial_{2}-1)+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}+X_{1}X_{2}\partial_{2}+1-\partial_{2})}.

(4.34)

Similarly, we can represent (
4.31
) in
\mathcal{A}_{2}
by

\partial_{2}^{2}-2X_{1}X_{2}\partial_{2}^{2}-X_{1}\partial_{2}-1.

(4.35)

Proposition 4.26
.

The map

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}^{2}+(2X_{1}+2)\partial_{1}-X^{2}_{2}\partial_{2}^{2})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}

is a well-defined left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear surjection.

4.4.1.
Classical Bessel polynomials

Proposition 4.27
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the classical Bessel polynomial

y_{n}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{2^{k}(n-k)!\,k!}\,x^{k},

for
n\geq 0
and
y_{-1}(x)=1
,
is the image of
1
via the left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\begin{array}[]{rclll}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X^{2}\partial^{2}+2(X+2)\partial-n(n+1)\big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times Y_{n}(X)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}\end{array}

The following PDEs (delay-differential relations) and three-term recurrence are direct consequences of the module
\mathcal{Y}
, as well as Corollaries
4.23
and
4.24
with
N=\mathcal{O}_{d}
.

Theorem 4.28

((
Delay-differential equations
))
.

The classical Bessel polynomials
\{y_{n}(x)\}

[
32
, p. 19]
satisfy the following (well-known) formulae

\begin{split}&x^{2}y^{\prime}_{n-1}(x)-[(n-1)x-1]y_{n-1}(x)-y_{n-2}(x)=0,\\
&x^{2}y^{\prime}_{n-1}(x)-y_{n}(x)+(nx+1)y_{n-1}(x)=0.\end{split}

(4.36)

for all
n
and all
x
.

Proof.

This follows from the two generators (
4.32
) and (
4.33
) of the Bessel polynomial module
\mathcal{Y}
in the Definition
4.25
, follows by the manifestation of the analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{d}
as a
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module given by the Example
2.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.29

((
Three-term recurrence

[
32
]
, p. 18))
.

The
(y_{n})
satisfies the (well-known) three-term recurrence formula

y_{n+1}(x)-(2n+1)xy_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}n}(x)-y_{n-1}(x)=0.

(4.37)

or every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and every
x
.

Proof.

This either follows from the last corollary or directly from the Corollary
4.24
with the manifestation of the analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{d}
as a
\mathcal{A}_{1}
-module given by the Example
2.3
.
∎

4.4.2.
Difference Bessel polynomials

Proposition 4.30
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then the difference Bessel polynomial

y^{\Delta}_{n}(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(n+k)!}{2^{k}(n-k)!\,k!}\,(x)_{k},

where we define
y^{\Delta}_{-1}(x):=1
, is the image of
1
via the left
\mathcal{A}
-linear map

\begin{array}[]{rclll}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\big(X^{2}\partial^{2}+2(X+2)\partial-n(n+1)\big)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times Y_{n}(X)}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}.\end{array}

The following delay-differential relations and three-term recurrence are direct consequences of the module
\mathcal{Y}
, as well as Corollaries
4.23
and
4.24
with
M=\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
.

Theorem 4.31

((
Delay-difference equations
))
.

The difference Bessel polynomials
(y_{n})
satisfy the following two delay-difference equations

\begin{split}x(x-1)\Delta y^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-2)-(nx-x)y^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-1)+y^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x)-y^{\Delta}_{n-2}(x)=0,\\
x(x-1)\Delta y^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-2)-y^{\Delta}_{n}(x)+nxy^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-1)+y^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x)=0\end{split}

(4.38)

for every
n
and every
x
.

Proof.

∎

Proposition 4.32

((
Three-term recurrence
))
.

The difference Bessel polynomials
(y^{\Delta}_{n})
satisfy the three-term recurrence formula

y^{\Delta}_{n+1}(x)-x(2n+1)y^{\Delta}_{n}(x-1)-y^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x)=0

(4.39)

for every
n
and
x
.

4.5.
Characteristic of Bessel polynomial module

We change one of the two generators from the defining Bessel polynomial module (
4.34
) by the element from the three-term recursion (
4.35
) to obtain following theorem.

Theorem 4.33
.

The natural map

\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{2}((1-2X_{1}X_{2})\partial_{2}^{2}-X_{1}\partial_{2}-1)+\mathcal{A}_{2}(X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}-\partial_{2}+X_{1}X_{2}\partial_{2}+1)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}

is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear isomorphism.

Theorem 4.34
.

Let

\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta):=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}\langle\eta\rangle}{\langle\eta^{2}-1+2X_{1}X_{2}\rangle},

\mathcal{Y}(\eta)=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)(\eta^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}-X_{1}\partial_{2}-1)+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)(X_{1}^{2}\partial_{1}-\partial_{2}+X_{1}X_{2}\partial_{2}+1)}

and let

S:=C_{1}\,{\mathop{\rm E}}(\frac{1-\eta}{X_{1}})+C_{2}\,{\mathop{\rm E}}(\frac{1+\eta}{X_{1}})

for every choice of
C_{1},\,C_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, where
{\mathop{\rm E}}
denote the Weyl exponential introduced in Example
3.3
.
Then
\mathcal{Y}(\eta)
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)
-module and the map

\mathcal{Y}(\eta)\xrightarrow{\times S}\overline{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)/(\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)\partial_{2})},

(4.40)

is left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\eta)
-linear.

Since the proof of Theorem
4.34
is similar to that of Theorem
4.22
, so we place it in the Appendix
A.3
.

5.
Newton transformations

To handle more sophisticated generating functions from Bessel modules in relation to difference calculus, we would like to introduce a transformation that allows us to “transform” entities with respect to differential operators to difference operators under different sittings. An origin of the transform can be traced to spectral analysis and integral forms of gamma functions, see e.g.,
[
66
, §12.22]
:

\Gamma(x)=-\frac{1}{2i\sin\pi x}\int_{\infty}^{(0+)}e^{-\lambda}(-\lambda)^{x-1}d\lambda\hskip 10.00002pt(|\arg(-\lambda)|<\pi)

which is valid on
\mathbb{C}
except at negative integers including the origin, where the upper and lower limits denote the standard Hankel contour which starts from
+\infty
above the real-axis, then circles around the origin in the counter-clockwise direction before returns to
+\infty
below the real-axis. If we replace
x
by
-x
, then the integral becomes

\Gamma(-x)=\frac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin\pi x}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}d\lambda\hskip 10.00002pt(|\arg(\lambda)|<\pi)

(5.1)

where the integral contour starts at
-\infty
before the negative real-axis, then circle around the origin in the counter-clockwise direction, before returns to
-\infty
above negative real-axis.

Theorem 5.1

((
Newton transformations
))
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
be the space of entire functions endowed with the structure of a left
\mathcal{A}
-module as in Example
2.9
, and
\mathcal{O}_{d}
be the space of entire functions
f
with growth rate

|f(x)\,e^{mx}|\to 0\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ as }\hskip 10.00002ptx\to-\infty

for some
m<1
, endowed with the structure of a left
\mathcal{A}
-module as in Example
2.3
.
Then the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{N}:\mathcal{O}_{d}&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}\\
f&\mapsto&\dfrac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin(\pi x)\Gamma(-x)}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}\,f(s)\,e^{s}(-s)^{-x-1}\,ds\end{array}

(5.2)

is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear. This map is called the Newton transformation.

Proof.

We just need to verify that
\mathfrak{N}(\partial f)=\partial(\mathfrak{N}f)
and
\mathfrak{N}(Xf)=X(\mathfrak{N}f)
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}(\partial f)(x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin(\pi x)\Gamma(-x)}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}\,f^{\prime}(s)\,e^{s}(-s)^{-x-1}\,ds

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin(\pi x)\Gamma(-x)}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}\,f(s)[e^{s}(-s)^{-x-1}+(x+1)e^{s}(-s)^{-x-2}]\,ds

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{N}f)(x+1)-(\mathfrak{N}f)(x)=\partial(\mathfrak{N}f)(x);

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}(Xf)(x)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin(\pi x)\Gamma(-x)}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}\,sf(s)\,e^{s}(-s)^{-x-1}\,ds

\displaystyle=-\dfrac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin(\pi x)\Gamma(-x)}\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}\,f(s)\,e^{s}(-s)^{-x}\,ds

\displaystyle=x(\mathfrak{N}f)(x-1)=X(\mathfrak{N}f)(x).

∎

Theorem 5.2
.

The Newton transformation defined above is an injection.

Proof.

It follows from the injectivity of the Mellin transformation and the observation that the integral above is nothing but the Mellin transformation of
f
times the exponential function.
∎

Example 5.3
.

Let us revisit the Weyl exponential
{\mathop{\rm E}}(aX)
from Example
3.3
derived when finding a
\mathcal{A}
-linear map as a solution
S
to

\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times S}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}\partial.

Since it is known that
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{A}(\partial-a)\xrightarrow{\times{\mathop{\rm E}}(aX)}\mathcal{A}/\partial\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\times 1}\mathcal{O}_{d}
can be given by the classical function
e^{ax}
where
\mathcal{O}_{d}
endowed with the
\mathcal{A}
-module structure described in Example
2.3
. Then the Newton transformation above completes the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}/\mathcal{D}(\partial-a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times e^{ax}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\exp_{\Delta}(a;\,X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{N}}

where the space of analytic functions
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}
is endowed with a
\mathcal{A}
-module structure defined in Example
2.4
. It is straightforward to verify that

\mathfrak{N}:x^{n}\ \ \longmapsto\ \ x(x-1)\cdots(x-n+1),

(5.3)

for every integer
n
, so that the above commutative diagram gives the difference exponential function

\exp_{\Delta}(a;\,x):=(a+1)^{x}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{k}}{k!}\,x(x-1)\cdots(x-k+1).

converges absolutely for any complex
x
, if
|a|<1
directly. The
\exp_{\Delta}
was already derived in Example
3.30
.

Moreover, it is easy to see that

\mathfrak{N}:x^{\nu}\ \ \longmapsto\ \ \frac{\Gamma(x+1)}{\Gamma(x+1-\nu)}=(x)_{\nu},

(5.4)

for every
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
.

We shall apply similar rationale as the above example to derive generating functions for certain half-Bessel
modules with respect to difference operator to be considered in subsequent sections. In fact, similar transformations already appeared in the literature in different applications spread over many decades. See for example
[
3
]
, in integral form
[
62
, p. 5 (25)]
for Laguerre polynomials, and again in
[
30
, p. 401]
in ad hoc manners.

Remark 5.4
.

Variants of the Newton transformation are possible. For example, if
\mathcal{O}_{d}
in Theorem
5.1
is replaced by the space of entire functions
f
with growth rate such that

|f(x)\,e^{mx}|\to 0\mbox{ as }x\to\pm i\infty,

holds for every
m\in\mathbb{C}
, then one replaces the Hankel-type contour in the above Newton transformation by, for example,
Then the conclusion of Theorem
5.1
remains valid.

6.
Poisson-type generating functions for (difference) Bessel polynomials

We are ready to apply the Theorem
4.22
derived from the last section to recover the well-known (Poisson-type) generating function for classical Bessel polynomials
\theta_{n}(x)
and to derive new
difference Bessel polynomials

\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x)
in this section.

Theorem 6.1

((
Poisson transformation I
))
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{d}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
denote the space of sequences of analytic functions
(f_{n}(x))_{0}^{\infty}
with appropriately restricted growth rate be endowed with the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure defined in Example
2.15
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
denote the space of analytic functions be endowed with the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure as in the Theorem
3.24
. Then the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}&\to&\mathcal{O}_{dd}\\
(f_{n})&\mapsto&\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}\dfrac{t^{n}}{n!},\end{array}

(6.1)

called the “Poisson transformation” is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear map.

Proof.

This is a straightforward verification.
∎

For finite difference operator
\Delta
, we have the following analogue.

Theorem 6.2

((
Poisson transformation II
))
.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
denote the space of bilateral sequences of analytic functions
(f_{n}(x))
with appropriately restricted growth rate be endowed with the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure defined in Example
2.16
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
denote the space of analytic functions be endowed with the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure as in the Theorem
3.24
. Then the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{p}_{\Delta}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}&\to&\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}\\
(f_{n})&\mapsto&\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}\dfrac{t^{n}}{n!},\end{array}

(6.2)

called the “difference Poisson transformation” is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear map.

Remark 6.3
.

The left
\mathcal{A}
-module structure of
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
as in Example
2.5
is specifically designed so that the z-transform
\mathfrak{z}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}\to\mathcal{O}_{d}
in Theorem
2.6
is left
\mathcal{A}
-linear. In a similar way, the left
\mathcal{A}
-module structure of
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
as in Example
2.15
and Example
2.16
is specifically designed so that the above Poisson transforms are left
\mathcal{A}
-linear.

6.1.
Classical reverse Bessel polynomials
\theta_{n}(x)

Example 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho):=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}\langle\rho,{1}/{\rho}\rangle}{\langle\rho^{2}-1+2X_{2}\rangle}
be defined as in Theorem
4.22
, and

\Theta(\rho):=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)(-\rho^{2}\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2})+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)(X_{1}\partial_{1}+\rho^{2}\partial_{2}-1-X_{1})}.

Then
\Theta(\rho)
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-module. On the other hand, the space
\mathcal{O}_{2}
of two-variable analytic functions with appropriate growth rate endowed with

\begin{split}(\partial_{1}f)(x,t))&=f_{x}(x,t),\quad(X_{1}f)(x,t)=xf(x,t),\\
(\partial_{2}f)(x,t)&=f_{t}(x,t),\quad(X_{2}f)(x,t)=tf(x,t)\end{split}

(6.3)

and by spectra analysis,
\rho^{2}f
can be defined so that

(\rho^{2}f)(x,t)=(1-2t)f(x,t),

\mathcal{O}_{2}
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-module again denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
, and the same space
\mathcal{O}_{2}
endowed with (
2.4
)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\partial_{1}f(x,\,t)=f(x+1,\,t)-f(x,\,t),&X_{1}f(x,\,t)=xf(x-1,\,t);\\
\partial_{2}f(x,\,t)=f_{t}(x,\,t),&X_{2}f(x,\,t)=tf(x,\,t),\end{array}

(6.4)

and

(\rho^{2}f)(x,t)=(1-2t)f(x,t),

is a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-module again denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
; and the space
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of all sequences of analytic functions endowed with (
2.8
) and
\rho^{2}f
can be defined so that

(\rho^{2}f)_{n}=f_{n}-2nf_{n-1}

(6.5)

is also a left
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-module.

We are ready to state our first main result in this subsection for which the generating function of the classical reverse Bessel polynomials derived by Burchnall
[
8
]
is a special case.

Theorem 6.5
.

(1)

Let
(\vartheta_{n})
be a sequence of analytic functions which is a solution of the reverse Bessel polynomial module
\Theta
in
\mathcal{O}_{d}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
. Then there exist complex constants
C_{1},\,C_{2}
such that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\Big\{C_{1}\exp\big[x(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]+C_{2}\exp\big[x(1+\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\Big\}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\vartheta_{n}(x)\,\frac{t^{n}}{n!}

(6.6)

which converges uniformly in each compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\times B(0,\,\frac{1}{2})
.

(2)

Moreover, the generating function in the part
\mathrm{(i)}
above satisfies the system of partial differential equations

\begin{split}&f_{xt}(x,\,t)-f_{t}(x,\,t)+xf(x,\,t)=0,\\
&xf_{x}(x,\,t)+(1-2t)f_{t}(x,\,t)-(1+x)f(x,\,t)=0\end{split}

(6.7)

for all
x
and
t
.
24
24

24

See also the Appendix
C.5
.
.

We skip the proof to this theorem since it is very similar to that of the following special case.

Theorem 6.6

((
[
32
]
, p. 42))
.

(1)

The reverse Bessel polynomials
(\theta_{n})
have the generating function

\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[x(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\theta_{n}(x)\,\frac{t^{n}}{n!}

(6.8)

which converges uniformly in each compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\times B(0,\,\frac{1}{2})
.

(2)

Moreover, the generating function in the part
\mathrm{(i)}
above satisfies the system of partial differential equations (
6.7
)
25
25

25

See also the Appendix
C.5
.
.

Proof.

Recall the map
\mathfrak{p}
as defined in (
6.1
) and the Example
6.4
just discussed above that
\Theta(\rho)
is a
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-modules above. Then we have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A}_{2}(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{dd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times(\theta_{n}(x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\{C_{1}\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1-\rho)]+C_{2}\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1+\rho)]\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 1}

(6.9)

in which
\widetilde{A}_{2}(\rho):=\overline{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)/\big[\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)\partial_{2}\big]}
. Since the above diagram commutes, the sum
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\theta_{n}(x)\,t^{n}/n!
being the image of
1
in
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
via the top-right path, is also a solution to the system of differential-difference equations

\begin{split}&\theta_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\theta_{n}(x)+x\theta_{n-1}(x)=0,\\
&x\theta_{n}^{\prime}(x)-(x+2n+1)\theta_{n}(x)+\theta_{n-1}(x)=0.\end{split}

(6.10)

mentioned in the Theorem
4.15
. Now the function
\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[x(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]
is a solution to the system of PDEs (
6.7
)

\displaystyle f_{xt}(x,\,t)-f_{t}(x,\,t)+xf(x,\,t)=0,

(6.11)

\displaystyle xf_{x}(x,\,t)+(1-2t)f_{t}(x,\,t)-(1+x)f(x,\,t)=0.

(6.12)

Since the PDEs (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) are images of the two generators of the Bessel module
\Theta
in Definition
4.9
, so the dimension of the local solution space of the PDEs coincides with the multiplicity two as stated in Theorem
4.10
.The Theorem
4.22
directly verifies that the solution space of the PDEs has
\mathbb{C}
-dimension two by solving the explicit solution as a linear combination of
\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1-\rho)]
and
\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1+\rho)]
of
\Theta(\rho)
in
\tilde{A}_{2}(\rho)
that effects the bottom-left path in the commutative diagram (
6.9
).
So

\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\big\{C_{1}\exp\big[x(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]+C_{2}\exp\big[x(1+\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\big\}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\theta_{n}(x)\,\frac{t^{n}}{n!}

for complex scalars
C_{1},\,C_{2}
. Substituting
t=0
into the above equation yields
C_{1}=1
and
C_{2}=0
. One way to verify the convergence of (
6.8
) is to combine (
6.11
) and (
6.12
) into a single ODE. Subtracting the equation (
6.11
) from (
6.12
) after it being differentiated partially with respect to
t
yields the ODE

(1-2t)f_{tt}(x,\,t)-3f_{t}(x,\,t)-x^{2}f(x,\,t)=0.

For each fixed, but otherwise arbitrary,
x
the ODE has a regular singularity at
t=\frac{1}{2}
. Hence the infinite sum in (
6.8
) converges uniformly in each compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\times B(0,\,\frac{1}{2})
. This completes the proof. ∎

6.2.
Difference reverse Bessel polynomials
\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)

Example 6.7
.

Let
\Theta(\rho)
and

(\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x))
to denote the Bessel polynomial module defined in Example
6.4
and infinite sequence of difference Bessel polynomials respectively. Suppose that
\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}_{\Delta}
is endowed with the
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-module structure as defined in Example
2.16
.
Then the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}:\Theta(\rho)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\theta^{\Delta}_{n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}_{\Delta}\end{array}

(6.13)

is left
\mathcal{A}_{2}
-linear.

We are now ready to make use of the Example
6.4
and to apply
Newton’s transform
\mathfrak{N}
to “map” the classical generating for the reverse Bessel polynomials derived in the last subsection to the difference reverse Bessel polynomials.

Theorem 6.8
.

(1)

The difference reverse Bessel polynomials have the generating function

\frac{e^{-i\pi x}}{2i\sin\pi x\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x)\,\frac{t^{n}}{n!},

(6.14)

where the infinite series converges uniformly in each compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\times B(0,\,\frac{1}{2})
.

(2)

Moreover, the generating function in part
\mathrm{(i)}
above satisfies the delay-differential equations
26
26

26

See also the Appendix
C.5
.

\begin{split}&f_{t}(x+1,\,t)-2f_{t}(x,\,t)+xf(x-1,\,t)=0,\\
&(1-2t)f_{t}(x,\,t)+(x-1)f(x,\,t)-2xf(x-1,\,t)=0.\end{split}

(6.15)

Proof.

We first draw on the simple fact from the Example
6.4

that
\Theta(\rho)
is a
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-module and from the Example
6.7
that the map
\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}:\Theta(\rho)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times(\theta^{\Delta}_{n})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}_{\Delta}
is
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-linear. Then
the map
\mathfrak{p}_{\Delta}:\mathcal{O}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
as defined in (
6.2
) and their composition is also an
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)
-linear. Thus
\mathfrak{p}_{\Delta}\circ\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}
acting on the identity yields the right-side of (
6.14
). Moreover, we have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{d}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{dd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times\rho^{-1}{\mathop{\rm E}}[X_{1}(1-\rho)]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{p}_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{N}}

(6.16)

in which
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{2}(\rho):=\overline{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)/\big[\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)\partial_{1}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(\rho)\partial_{2}\big]}
, and the growth of the analytic functions in
\mathcal{O}_{dd}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
are suitably restricted. Let

y(x,t)=\int_{-\infty}^{(0+)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda.

Since
\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[x(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]
is a solution to the system of PDEs (

) according to the leftmost path of the above diagram, this

satisfies

\begin{split}&[\partial_{2}\partial_{1}-\partial_{2}+X_{1}]y(x,\,t)=0,\\
&\big[X_{1}\partial_{2}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}\big]y(x,\,t)=0,\end{split}

by Theorem

in the analytic function space
\mathcal{O}_{\Delta d}
endowed with a

-module structure given by the Example

. Hence the

satisfies the system of delay-differential equations (

). We instead offer an alternative approach by direct verification that the
y(x,\,t)
to satisfies the equivalent system
27
27

27

This would make the verification shorter.

\begin{split}&[(2X_{1}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}]y(x,\,t)=0,\\
&\big[X_{1}\partial_{1}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}\big]y(x,\,t)=0,\end{split}

where the first expression above can be found in Proposition
4.11
.
It is straightforward to note that

\partial_{2}y=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\big[(1-2t)^{-1}+\lambda(1-2t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big]\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda

and

\begin{split}\partial^{2}_{2}y&=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\big[3(1-2t)^{-2}+3\lambda(1-2t)^{-\frac{3}{2}}+\lambda^{2}(1-2t)^{-1}\big]\\
&\quad\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda\\
\end{split}

so that

\begin{split}(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}y=&\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\big[-3(1-2t)^{-1}-3\lambda(1-2t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\lambda^{2}\big]\\
&\quad\times\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda\\
&=(-3\partial_{2}-X_{1}^{2})y.\end{split}

Hence

\big[(2X_{2}-1)\partial_{2}^{2}+3\partial_{2}+X_{1}^{2}\big]y=0.

On the other hand, we have

X_{1}\partial_{1}y=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}(x-\lambda)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda\\

and

\begin{split}\big[(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}\big]y&=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x}(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda\\
=&\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}d\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big].\end{split}

Integration-by-parts of the above improper integral over an Hankel-type contour yields

\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\Big|_{-\infty}^{(0+)}=0

so that

\begin{split}\big[(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}\big]y=&-\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d(e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x})\\
=&-X_{1}\partial_{1}\frac{1}{\Gamma(-x)}\int_{-\infty}^{(+0)}e^{\lambda}(-\lambda)^{-x-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2t}}\exp\big[\lambda(1-\sqrt{1-2t})\big]\,d\lambda.\end{split}

Hence

\big[X_{1}\partial_{1}+(1-2X_{2})\partial_{2}-1-X_{1}\big]y=0

as desired.
That is,
y(x,\,t)
satisfies

\displaystyle(2t-1)f_{tt}(x,\,t)+3f_{t}(x,t)+x(x-1)f(x-2,t)=0,

(6.17)

\displaystyle(1-2t)f_{t}(x,t)+(x-1)f(x,t)-2xf(x-1,t)=0.

(6.18)

Hence
y(x,\,t)
satisfies the original system of delay-differential equations (
6.15
).

Let us return to the right-half of the commutative diagram above. We notice that the rightmost path of the commutative diagram (
6.16
), the sum
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\theta_{n}^{\Delta}(x)\,t^{n}/n!
is a (formal sum) solution to the system of delay-difference equations

\displaystyle\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x+1)-2\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)+x\theta^{\Delta}_{n-1}(x-1)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\theta^{\Delta}_{n+1}(x)+(x-2n-3)\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x)-2x\,\theta^{\Delta}_{n}(x-1)

\displaystyle=0

for each
n
, which were
derived in the Theorem
4.17
.

Let us determine the region of convergence of (
6.14
). First we differentiate the equation (
6.18
) with respect to
t
. This yields

(1-2t)f_{tt}(x,\,t)+(x-3)f_{t}(x,\,t)-2xf_{t}(x-1,\,t)=0.

(6.19)

Replace
x
by
x-1
in equation (
6.18
) and multiply the resulting equation by
x
throughout yield

x(1-2t)f_{t}(x-1,t)+x(x-2)f(x-1,t)-2x(x-1)f(x-2,\,t)=0.

(6.20)

Multiply the equation (
6.17
) throughout by
2
and added to the (
6.20
) yield

2(2t-1)f_{tt}(x,t)+6f_{t}(x,t)+x(1-2t)f_{t}(x-1,t)+x(x-2)f(x-1,t)=0.

(6.21)

Multiply the equation (
6.19
) throughout by
1-2t
yields

(1-2t)^{2}f_{tt}(x,t)+(1-2t)(x-3)f_{t}(x,t)-2(1-2t)xf_{t}(x-1,t)=0.

(6.22)

Multiply the equation (
6.21
) by
2
and add the resulting equation to (
6.22
) yield

[(1-2t)^{2}+4(2t-1)]f_{tt}(x,t)+[(1-2t)(x-3)+12]f_{t}(x,t)+2x(x-2)f(x-1,t)=0.

(6.23)

Now multiply the equation (

) throughout by the factor
x-2
yields

(x-2)(1-2t)f_{t}(x,t)+(x-2)(x-1)f(x,t)-2x(x-2)f(x-1,t)=0.

(6.24)

It is now clear that the result of adding (

) and (
6.24
) yields, after simplification, the equation

(6.25)

For a fixed
x
, the above equation (
6.25
) is a second order linear ODE with two finite regular singularities and
t=\frac{1}{2}
is the one closest to the origin
t=0
which is an ordinary point. Hence we conclude that the expansion in (
6.14
) converges uniformly in each compact subset of
\mathbb{C}\times B(0,\,\frac{1}{2})
as asserted.
This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎
